### Tool result

_source line 3203 · 2026-05-17T17:39:34.829970+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this safety commitment in real time requires comparison of hidden-state (HS) and text-based monitors, yet no prior work systematically compares these modalities or examines how layer selection methodology affects the outcome. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal safety probing across four reasoning models, two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). We discover that layer selection metric bias entirely determines whether an HS advantage appears: a natural composite metric systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale and masks the HS signal. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm--Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15.4 to +23.6 percentage points (pp) across all four models. Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities. Six alternative approaches (BOCPD/CUSUM change-point detection, Mahalanobis distance, Representation Engineering, MLP probes, CCS, cross-model transfer) all fail, which establishes clear boundaries for current methods. Control task selectivity of 7.5--18.3pp above chance confirms genuine learned safety structure.
2

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work provides the first systematic evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models that span two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
4	The central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion.
5	A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage and producing the appearance of a scale-dependent effect.
6	When layers are selected by balanced accuracy alone, four independent metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\% depth for 32B models), and all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant hidden-state superiority, with precision gains of +15.4 to +23.6pp across all four models.
7	Six alternative approaches fail to match supervised linear probes: BOCPD and CUSUM cannot detect gradual commitment transitions, Mahalanobis distance misses signal concentrated in minor principal components, Representation Engineering yields model-dependent results, MLP probes overfit at 32B sample sizes, CCS provides no gain over standard probing, and cross-model transfer collapses due to model-specific safety representations.
8	These negative results collectively confirm that safety commitment is gradual, model-specific, and invisible to geometric or unsupervised methods.
9	
10	For practitioners and future probing studies, we recommend temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) paired with principled layer selection based on pure balanced accuracy as robust defaults.
11	Layer selection sensitivity analysis should become standard practice: without it, composite metrics that embed crossing rate can silently bias conclusions toward shallow layers where positional features dominate.

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{R1-32B statistical narrowness.}
4	Only $W{=}1$ passes Bonferroni correction for R1-32B, covering 25\% of the window range (5 of 20 traces per fold). The dimensionality constraint of $51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ traces prevents reliable evaluation at larger window sizes. Whether collecting more training traces would resolve this statistical limitation remains unknown.
5	
6	\paragraph{QwQ-32B timing limitation.}
7	On QwQ-32B, hidden-state probes achieve higher accuracy than text probes but detect commitment later: HS lead time is negative ($-1.2$ to $+1.9$ steps) compared to text lead time of $3.7$--$12.3$ steps. A position-only baseline achieves comparable balanced accuracy (0.746 vs.\ HS 0.740), suggesting that much of the detection signal on this model is positional rather than representational. The practical value of marginally higher accuracy with later detection depends on the deployment context.
8	
9	\paragraph{High safe-trace false positive rate.}
10	All probing methods produce 39--62\% false positive rates on safe traces at standard operating thresholds. Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon as a research tool rather than constitute production-ready detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and active intervention shows that early detection via passive probing captures only a fraction of the theoretically available safety signal.
11	
12	\paragraph{Scale and architecture coverage.}
13	We evaluate 4 models spanning 2 architectures (Qwen-2.5, LLaMA-3.1) and 2 parameter scales (7--8B, 32B). Intermediate scales (13B, 70B) that would test whether the observed layer-selection pattern interpolates smoothly are missing. We also lack evaluation on proprietary reasoning models (e.g., o1, o3), where chain-of-thought structure may differ substantially.
14	
15	\paragraph{Text encoder as baseline.}
16	Text probes use a single 384-dimensional encoder (all-MiniLM-L6-v2).

_source line 3215 · 2026-05-17T17:40:04.962431+00:00_

25:\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the biased layers, HS vs.\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the corrected deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\cref{sec:layer-results}).

23:\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: all five metrics agree on layer~14 (44\% depth). OT-7B: biased metric selects L0 (0\%), unbiased consensus selects L16 (57\%). QwQ-32B: biased metric selects L2 (3\%), unbiased consensus selects L60 (94\%). R1-32B: biased metric selects L10 (16\%), unbiased consensus selects L63 (98\%). The bias is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the composite metric selects layers in the bottom 16\% of the network while all other metrics select layers above 94\%.
258:\paragraph{Procedure.} For each model, we randomly permute the binary labels (pre-CP vs. post-CP) across all steps while preserving the marginal label distribution. We then retrain the probe with identical architecture (L2-regularized logistic regression) and hyperparameters on these shuffled labels. Selectivity is defined as:

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
4	\label{app:checklist}
5	
6	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
7	
8	\begin{itemize}
9	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
10	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark.
11	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
12	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations. No new data collection was performed.
13	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
14	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
15	\end{itemize}
16	
17	
18	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
19	\label{app:layer_sweep}
20	
21	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the biased composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The biased composite systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. The four unbiased metrics converge on deep layers for all models.
22

249	\paragraph{OT-7B multi-layer GRU (layers 13--19, 3 seeds).}
250	OT-7B GRU achieves 74.6$\pm$1.3\% balanced accuracy, 64.5$\pm$7.0\% coverage, +33.6$\pm$4.8 steps lead, and 27.4$\pm$6.1\% pre-CP FPR. The lead time is substantially longer than other models in absolute terms, but OT-7B traces average ${\sim}$80 reasoning steps (vs.\ ${\sim}$30 for R1-8B and 32B models), so absolute lead times are not directly comparable. The FPR-lead tradeoff mirrors R1-8B: GRU FPR (27.4\%) is far above the LR probe at layer 16 (10.0\%), confirming that GRUs gain lead time at the cost of more false alarms across both 7-8B models.
251	
252	
253	\section{Control Task Methodology}
254	\label{app:control}
255	
256	We follow the selectivity framework of \cite{hewitt2019control} to verify that our probes capture genuine safety-relevant structure rather than surface-level correlations.
257	
258	\paragraph{Procedure.} For each model, we randomly permute the binary labels (pre-CP vs. post-CP) across all steps while preserving the marginal label distribution. We then retrain the probe with identical architecture (L2-regularized logistic regression) and hyperparameters on these shuffled labels. Selectivity is defined as:
259	\begin{equation}
260	    \text{Selectivity} = \text{Acc}_{\text{linguistic}} - \text{Acc}_{\text{control}}
261	\end{equation}
262	where $\text{Acc}_{\text{linguistic}}$ is the probe accuracy on true labels and $\text{Acc}_{\text{control}}$ is the accuracy on permuted labels. A selectivity of zero indicates that the probe exploits only non-linguistic features (e.g., position); higher values confirm that the representation encodes task-relevant information.
263	
264	\paragraph{Results.} We repeat the control experiment 5 times (10 for OT-7B) with different random permutations and report the mean control accuracy. Selectivity scores are: OT-7B 18.3pp (layer 16), QwQ-32B 15.3pp (layer 2), R1-8B 12.5pp (layer 14), R1-32B 7.5pp (layer 10). All values exceed the 5pp thresho
…[truncated 12512 chars]

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Results.} All 18 transfer configurations (6 directional pairs $\\times$ 3 methods: PCA-512, PCA-1024, PCA-2048, plus direct for same-dimension pairs) fail to produce meaningful transfer. The best result is QwQ-32B $\\rightarrow$ R1-32B direct transfer at 67.3\\% balanced accuracy, against a within-model baseline of 65.2\\% (+2.1pp). The complete failure under maximally favorable conditions (PCA fitted on full data, no train/test separation for projection) strengthens the conclusion that safety commitment is encoded in model-specific geometric configurations. PCA is a standard linear alignment baseline; its failure indicates that the problem lies in the representations themselves rather than in the choice of alignment method.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Results.} All 17 transfer configurations (5 directional pairs $\\times$ 3 PCA dimensionalities, plus 2 direct transfers for same-dimension pairs) fail to produce practically useful transfer. The best PCA result is R1-8B $\\rightarrow$ QwQ-32B at 59.5\\% balanced accuracy (PCA-2048). The best direct transfer (QwQ-32B $\\rightarrow$ R1-32B) reaches 67.3\\% balanced accuracy, which exceeds the within-model baseline of 65.2\\%. However, this transfer direction achieves only 1\\% crossing rate, meaning the transferred probe almost never triggers detection in practice, rendering it useless for deployment despite above-baseline accuracy. The complete failure under maximally favorable conditions (PCA fitted on full data, no train/test separation for projection) strengthens the conclusion that safety commitment is encoded in model-specific geometric configurations. PCA is a standard linear alignment baseline; its failure indicates that the problem lies in the representations themselves rather than in the choice of alignment method."
}

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
4	\label{app:checklist}
5	
6	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
7	
8	\begin{itemize}
9	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
10	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark.
11	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
12	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations. No new data collection was performed.
13	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
14	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
15	\end{itemize}
16	
17	
18	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
19	\label{app:layer_sweep}
20	
21	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the biased composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The biased composite systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. The four unbiased metrics converge on deep layers for all models.
22	
23	\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: all five metrics agree on layer~14 (44\% depth). OT-7B: biased m

Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m{=}12$) must satisfy $p_i < \\alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure. All 12 tests pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$, controlling the family-wise error rate.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 hypothesis tests (4 models $\\times$ 3 metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All tests pass at $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}\n\\begin{tabular}{clllcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Rank} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{$p$-value} & \\textbf{Threshold} & \\textbf{Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1  & R1-8B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0042 & \\checkmark \\\\\n2  & OT-7B  & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0045 & \\checkmark \\\\\n3  & OT-7B  & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0050 & \\checkmark \\\\\n4  & OT-7B  & FPR & $<$0.001 & 0.0056 & \\checkmark \\\\\n5  & QwQ-32B & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0063 & \\checkmark \\\\\n6  & QwQ-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0071 & \\checkmark \\\\\n7  & R1-32B & Bal.\\ Acc. & $<$0.001 & 0.0083 & \\checkmark \\\\\n8  & R1-32B & Precision & $<$0.001 & 0.0100 & \\checkmark \\\\\n9  & QwQ-32B & FPR & 0.004 & 0.0125 & \\checkmark \\\\\n10 & R1-8B  & Bal.\\ Acc. & 0.005 & 0.0167 & \\checkmark \\\\\n11 & R1-32B & FPR & 0.007 & 0.0250 & \\checkmark \\\\\n12 & R1-8B  & FPR & 0.044 & 0.0500 & \\checkmark \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe weakest test (rank 12, R1-8B FPR, $p{=}0.044$) passes its threshold ($\\alpha/1 = 0.05$) with margin. The ordering reveals that OT-7B produces the strongest HS-text separation (ranks 2--4), while R1-8B FPR is the least significant despite R1-8B showing the largest precision gap (+22.4pp). This reflects R1-8B's already-low text FPR (11.8\\%), which leaves less room for HS improvement on this metric.\n\n\n\\section{Window Size Ablation Details}\n\\label{app:window_ablation}"
}

_source line 3271 · 2026-05-17T17:46:19.624183+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification. \citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. Both operate on generated text and assume that safety violations are linguistically explicit in the CoT. These works collectively characterize the problem or propose text-level interventions; none conducts a systematic modality comparison to determine whether hidden states or text better supports commitment detection.
5	
6	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
7	A parallel line of work uses internal model activations to detect safety violations. TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial mani

15	    \item \textbf{Universal HS advantage at correctly selected layers.} At deep layers selected by unbiased metrics, HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models (12/12 Holm-Bonferroni at $\alpha{=}0.05$, precision +15.4 to +23.6pp; \cref{sec:hs-text}, \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Model-specific trade-offs at 32B qualify the practical implications: R1-32B provides excellent early detection (+13 steps lead time) but narrow coverage at the significant temporal window, while QwQ shows robust significance across all 15 window sizes but later detection than text (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Comprehensive negative results define method boundaries.} Six alternative detection methods fail (\cref{sec:negative}, \cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD and CUSUM produce no early-detection gain because commitment is gradual rather than abrupt; Mahalanobis distance fails because safe and jailbreak trajectories occupy indistinguishable activation regions; RepE mean-difference directions show inconsistent performance at deep layers (failing on R1-32B at 0.559 while approaching supervised accuracy on QwQ at 0.756), but consistently underperform supervised probes on precision and FPR across all models; MLP probes overfit on small datasets, underperforming linear probes on 3 of 4 models; CCS~\cite{burns2023discovering} provides no gain over supervised probing; and cross-model transfer fails entirely, confirming that safety representations are model-specific. Control task selectivity (7.5--18.3pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms that probes capture genuine safety structure rather than positional artifacts.
18	\end{itemize}

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, all 12 tests pass. The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency risks the same systematic bias toward early layers. This bias is not specific to safety probing. Any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two independent layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect layer selection methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.
5	
6	\paragraph{Temporal context and modality choice.}
7	Information-theoretic reasoning suggests that hidden states (4096 to 5120 dimensions of internal computation) should dominate text features (384-dimensional sentence embeddings) purely by signal capacity. Both temporal context and modality choice significantly improve detection, but their relative magnitudes differ by scale. At 7--8B, the modality advanta

_source line 3291 · 2026-05-17T17:47:31.303066+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work provides the first systematic evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models that span two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
4	The central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion.
5	A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage and producing the appearance of a scale-dependent effect.
6	When layers are selected by balanced accuracy alone, four independent metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\% depth for 32B models), and all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant hidden-state superiority, with precision gains of +15.4 to +23.6pp across all four models.
7	Six alternative approaches fail to match supervised linear probes: BOCPD and CUSUM cannot detect gradual commitment transitions, Mahalanobis distance misses signal concentrated in minor principal components, Representation Engineering yields model-dependent results, MLP probes overfit at 32B sample sizes, CCS provides no gain over standard probing, and cross-model transfer collapses due to model-specific safety representations.
8	These negative results collectively confirm that safety commitment is gradual, model-specific, and invisible to geometric or unsupervised methods.
9	
10	For practitioners and future probing studies, we recommend temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) paired with principled layer selection based on pure balanced accuracy as robust defaults.
11	Layer selection sensitivity analysis should become standard practice: without it, composite metrics that embed crossing rate can silently bias conclusions toward shallow layers where positional features dominate.
12	At 32B scale, per-model validation is essential because models with identical architecture but different t

205	OT-7B text probes improve monotonically from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$ (+3.3pp accuracy, +8.5pp precision, $-$18.4pp FPR), following the same pattern as R1-8B text probes. OT-7B HS probes show a split pattern: balanced accuracy peaks at $W{=}3$ and declines, while precision and FPR continue improving (\cref{tab:window_full}). The text-HS contrast is therefore metric-dependent rather than a blanket modality difference.
206	
207	\paragraph{Bootstrap comparison (matched $W{=}3$).} To ensure a fair modality comparison, bootstrap tests use matched $W{=}3$ for both HS and text probes. At $W{=}3$, HS probes achieve 75.3\% trace-level balanced accuracy vs.\ 65.6\% for text (bootstrap $\Delta{=}$+10.7pp, 95\% CI [4.9, 16.3], $p{<}0.001$, survives Holm-Bonferroni). Precision: 55.8\% vs.\ 34.6\% (bootstrap $\Delta{=}$+21.9pp, 95\% CI [14.0, 29.6], $p{<}0.001$, survives Holm-Bonferroni). Pre-CP FPR: 10.0\% vs.\ 27.1\% (bootstrap $\Delta{=}{-}17.1$pp, 95\% CI [$-$22.2, $-$12.2], $p{<}0.001$, survives Holm-Bonferroni). All three metrics survive correction. The point-estimate precision gap (+21.2pp) closely matches R1-8B (+22.4pp); the bootstrap mean (+21.9pp) differs slightly due to resampling variance. Using each modality's optimal $W$ (HS at $W{=}3$, text at $W{=}25$) would introduce a confound because text would benefit from 8$\times$ more temporal context.

_source line 3305 · 2026-05-17T17:49:06.052826+00:00_

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{R1-32B statistical narrowness.}
4	Only $W{=}1$ passes Bonferroni correction for R1-32B, covering 25\% of the window range (5 of 20 traces per fold). The dimensionality constraint of $51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ traces prevents reliable evaluation at larger window sizes. Whether collecting more training traces would resolve this statistical limitation remains unknown.
5	
6	\paragraph{QwQ-32B timing limitation.}
7	On QwQ-32B, hidden-state probes achieve higher accuracy than text probes but detect commitment later: HS lead time is negative ($-1.2$ to $+1.9$ steps) compared to text lead time of $3.7$--$12.3$ steps. A position-only baseline achieves comparable balanced accuracy (0.746 vs.\ HS 0.740), suggesting that much of the detection signal on this model is positional rather than representational. The practical value of marginally higher accuracy with later detection depends on the deployment context.
8	
9	\paragraph{High safe-trace false positive rate.}
10	All probing methods produce 39--62\% false positive rates on safe traces at standard operating thresholds. Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon as a research tool rather than constitute production-ready detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and active intervention shows that early detection via passive probing captures only a fraction of the theoretically available safety signal.
11	
12	\paragraph{Scale and architecture coverage.}
13	We evaluate 4 models spanning 2 architectures (Qwen-2.5, LLaMA-3.1) and 2 parameter scales (7--8B, 32B). Intermediate scales (13B, 70B) that would test whether the observed layer-selection pattern interpolates smoothly are missing. We also lack evaluation on proprietary reasoning models (e.g., o1, o3), where chain-of-thought structure may differ substantially.
14	
15	\paragraph{Text encoder as baseline.}
16	Text probes use a single 384-dimensional encoder (all-MiniLM-L6-v2).

_source line 3317 · 2026-05-17T17:49:48.864720+00:00_

[Director 指令] [URGENT Patch — 提交前阻塞项，优先级最高]

两个 critical 修复，其他所有 pending patches 之前先做这两个：

## 修复 1: main.tex 加入 discussion.tex

当前 main.tex 在 `\input{sections/experiments}` 之后直接跳到 `\input{sections/conclusion}`，缺少 discussion。

修改：在 `\input{sections/experiments}` 和 `\input{sections/conclusion}` 之间加入：
```
\input{sections/discussion}
```

## 修复 2: abstract.tex 全面重写

当前 abstract 仍是旧 framing（Story B: scale-dependent, 5/12 HB, OT-7B +21.2pp）。必须重写为 Story A（layer selection as primary, 12/12 HB）。

**替换为以下内容（直接覆盖整个 abstract.tex）**：

```
Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this \emph{safety commitment} in real time is essential for monitoring systems, yet no prior work has systematically compared hidden-state (HS) probes against text-based monitors for this task. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal probing for safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our primary methodological finding is that layer selection methodology can reverse a study's conclusions: the composite metric \texttt{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm--Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15 to +24 percentage points (pp) across all four models. Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities, providing the most robust single default for safety monitoring. Six alternative approaches (change-point detection, geometric distance, Representation Engineering, CCS, nonlinear probes, cross-model transfer) all fail, establishing clear boundaries of current methods. Control tasks confirm genuine safety-specific structure (selectivity 7.5--18.3pp above chance).
```

**核心改动说明**：
- 12/12 HB（不是 5/12）
- +15 to +24pp 跨 4 模型（不是只报 2 个 7-8B 模型）
- Layer selection as primary finding（不是 scale-dependent）
- 删除 "scale-dependent" 叙事
- OT-7B 数字不再单独列出（headline 用 range）

完成后立即回报。这两个修复完成后我提交 Review Director 最终审查。

_source line 3321 · 2026-05-17T17:49:59.749683+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this safety commitment in real time requires comparison of hidden-state (HS) and text-based monitors, yet no prior work systematically compares these modalities or examines how layer selection methodology affects the outcome. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal safety probing across four reasoning models, two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). We discover that layer selection metric bias entirely determines whether an HS advantage appears: a natural composite metric systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale and masks the HS signal. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm--Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15.4 to +23.6 percentage points (pp) across all four models. Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities. Six alternative approaches (BOCPD/CUSUM change-point detection, Mahalanobis distance, Representation Engineering, MLP probes, CCS, cross-model transfer) all fail, which establishes clear boundaries for current methods. Control task selectivity of 7.5--18.3pp above chance confirms genuine learned safety structure.
2

_source line 3345 · 2026-05-17T17:53:24.086839+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this \emph{safety commitment} in real time is essential for monitoring systems, yet no prior work has systematically compared hidden-state (HS) probes against text-based monitors for this task. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal probing for safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our primary methodological finding is that layer selection methodology can reverse a study's conclusions: the composite metric \texttt{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm--Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15 to +24 percentage points (pp) across all four models. Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities, providing the most robust single default for safety monitoring. Six alternative approaches (change-point detection, geometric distance, Representation Engineering, CCS, nonlinear probes, cross-model transfer) all fail, establishing clear boundaries of current methods. Control tasks confirm genuine safety-specific structure (selectivity 7.5--18.3pp above chance).
2

_source line 3347 · 2026-05-17T17:53:24.338404+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT), producing dozens of internal reasoning steps before generating a final response. This deliberative process interleaves safety-aware reasoning with content generation across many steps, and the very capacity for self-reflection introduces a failure mode absent from standard instruction-tuned models. Recent analyses have documented that reasoning models can ``self-jailbreak'': without any adversarial modification to the input prompt, the model's own CoT transitions from considering safety constraints to committing to produce harmful content~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. In a typical self-jailbreak trace, the model begins by acknowledging that a request may be harmful, engages in several steps of safety deliberation, and then gradually shifts toward compliance before abandoning safety reasoning entirely. We refer to the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). Before the CP, the model is still weighing refusal; after it, harmful generation proceeds unchecked. This failure mode is qualitatively different from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs: the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning rather than from a crafted input. Detecting this transition in real time is essential for monitoring systems that must intervene before harmful content reaches users, yet no existing detector was designed for this internal commitment process.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target a fundamentally different problem. Hidden-state (HS) probing methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} monitor internal activations to classify adversarial inputs directed at instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs), achieving above 95\% defense rates on standard b

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria. Our experiments address three questions: (1)~Does the layer selection metric determine whether HS probes appear effective? (2)~Do HS probes outperform text probes at correctly selected layers? (3)~Which alternative detection methods fail, and why?
4	
5	\subsection{Experimental Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Models and data.} We study four models spanning two architectures and two parameter scales: R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (Qwen-7B, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level commitment point (CP) annotations. We use a 60/20/20 train/validation/test split across all models (\cref{tab:models}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Training and evaluation.} All probes use L2-regularized logistic regression with balanced class weights. For each model, we select the temporal window $W$ on the validation set from $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$. HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected transformer layer; text probes encode each reasoning step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384 dimensions). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap test (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction across 12 tests (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models, $\alpha{=}0.05$).
10	
11	\paragraph{Layer selection.} We evaluate five layer selection metrics per model (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}). The biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because representational volatility at early layers inflates the crossing rate component. Four unbiased metrics (pure b

_source line 3351 · 2026-05-17T17:53:31.215525+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work provides the first systematic evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models that span two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B).
4	The central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion.
5	A composite metric that incorporates crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage and producing the appearance of a scale-dependent effect.
6	When layers are selected by balanced accuracy alone, four independent metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\% depth for 32B models), and all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant hidden-state superiority, with precision gains of +15.4 to +23.6pp across all four models.
7	Six alternative approaches fail to match supervised linear probes: BOCPD and CUSUM cannot detect gradual commitment transitions, Mahalanobis distance misses signal concentrated in minor principal components, Representation Engineering yields model-dependent results, MLP probes overfit at 32B sample sizes, CCS provides no gain over standard probing, and cross-model transfer collapses due to model-specific safety representations.
8	These negative results collectively confirm that safety commitment is gradual, model-specific, and invisible to geometric or unsupervised methods.
9	
10	For practitioners and future probing studies, we recommend temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) paired with principled layer selection based on pure balanced accuracy as robust defaults.
11	Layer selection sensitivity analysis should become standard practice: without it, composite metrics that embed crossing rate can silently bias conclusions toward shallow layers where positional features dominate.
12	At 32B scale, per-model validation is essential because models with identical architecture but different t
…[truncated 705 chars]

_source line 3353 · 2026-05-17T17:53:31.468776+00:00_

70	\subsection{Baseline Methods}
71	\label{sec:baselines}
72	
73	We evaluate six alternative detection approaches to contextualize probe performance and test whether simpler or unsupervised methods suffice.
74	
75	\paragraph{BOCPD.} Bayesian Online Change-Point Detection~\citep{adams2007bayesian} is applied to probe score sequences, testing whether the commitment point produces an abrupt distributional shift detectable without supervised labels. We sweep hazard rate and observation model parameters on the validation set.
76	
77	\paragraph{CUSUM.} Cumulative sum control charts accumulate evidence for a mean shift in the probe score sequence. We sweep the reference value $\delta$ and decision threshold $h$ to find optimal configurations per model (\cref{app:bocpd_config}).
78	
79	\paragraph{Mahalanobis distance.} Following TrajGuard~\citep{trajguard}, we compute the Mahalanobis distance of each step's hidden state from the pre-CP distribution centroid, testing whether geometric separation in activation space suffices for detection without training a discriminative probe.

53	
54	\paragraph{Five-metric ablation.} To quantify this bias, we evaluate layer selection under five metrics across all four models:
55	
56	\begin{enumerate}
57	\item \textbf{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} (biased composite): Selects layer 2 for QwQ-32B (3\% depth) and layer 10 for R1-32B (16\% depth).
58	\item \textbf{Pure balanced accuracy}: Selects deep layers consistently: layer 14 for R1-8B (44\%), layer 16 for OT-7B (57\%), layer 60 for QwQ-32B (94\%), layer 63 for R1-32B (98\%).
59	\item \textbf{Precision}: Converges on deep layers for all four models.
60	\item \textbf{Inverse pre-CP false positive rate (FPR)}: Converges on deep layers for all four models.
61	\item \textbf{Composite (bal\_acc + precision $-$ FPR)}: Converges on deep layers for all four models.
62	\end{enumerate}
63	
64	Four of five metrics independently converge on deep layers. Only the crossing-rate component of metric (1) drives shallow-layer selection. The full metric $\times$ model ablation is presented in \cref{tab:layer_selection}.

208	
209	
210	\section{GRU Training Details}
211	\label{app:gru_details}
212	
213	\paragraph{Architecture.} We evaluate two GRU variants. The single-layer variant uses a GRU with hidden dimension 256, taking per-step hidden states from the best layer (selected by accuracy-weighted layer selection) as input. The multi-layer variant concatenates hidden states from 7 layers (evenly spaced through the network) at each step, producing a higher-dimensional input to the same GRU architecture.
214	
215	\paragraph{Training procedure.} We train with Adam (learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, default $\beta_1{=}0.9$, $\beta_2{=}0.999$), batch size 16, for a maximum of 50 epochs. Early stopping halts training when validation loss does not improve for 10 consecutive epochs. Input features are z-score normalized per dimension using training set statistics.
216	
217	\paragraph{Evaluation.} We report crossing accuracy (the sustained accuracy at which the probe first exceeds chance for 5 consecutive steps) and detection lead (steps before CP at crossing point). All experiments use 3 random seeds; we report mean $\pm$ standard deviation.
218

  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work provides the first systematic cross-model evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures, two training methods, and two scales.\nThe central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion: a composite metric incorporating crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage, while four complementary metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\\% depth).\nAt correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected tests confirm significant HS superiority (precision +15.4 to +23.6pp), and seven alternative methods fail, though practical detection profiles diverge by model (R1-32B provides early warning at narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides broad significance with poor timing).\nClosing the 85.5\\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and the theoretical detection frontier will require causal intervention techniques such as activation patching, extension to multilingual and multi-turn settings, evaluation at intermediate scales (13B, 70B), and exploration of stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond the PCA-based and direct transfer approaches tested here.\n"
}
```

_source line 3379 · 2026-05-17T17:55:31.323483+00:00_

65	
66	\paragraph{Downstream impact.} The practical consequence of biased layer selection is severe. For the 32B models, selecting shallow layers entirely masks the hidden-state probe advantage over text probes: probe performance at layers 2--10 is statistically indistinguishable from text probes, while the same probes at layers 60--63 show clear separation. This demonstrates that layer selection is not a minor implementation detail but a methodological choice that can flip a paper's central conclusion.
67	
68	\paragraph{Why deep layers are correct.} Our claim that deep layers provide the appropriate selection does not rest on the circular argument that ``HS probes outperform text probes at deep layers.'' Three independent lines of evidence support this conclusion. First, the mechanistic analysis above identifies a specific, quantifiable bias in the crossing-rate metric that artificially inflates shallow-layer scores. Second, four of five selection metrics, each capturing a different aspect of probe quality, independently converge on deep layers. Third, the probing literature consistently finds that task-relevant semantic representations form in mid-to-deep transformer layers~\citep{belinkov2022probing, tenney2019bert}. The convergence of mechanistic reasoning, empirical metric agreement, and established probing theory provides a non-circular justification for deep-layer selection.
69

370	\paragraph{Impact on reported results.} All numbers reported in this paper use the corrected text embedding pipeline. The bug affected only QwQ-32B text probe features. The correction, combined with the separate layer selection correction (\cref{sec:layer-results}), means that early-stage intermediate results (non-significant 32B modality comparisons) reflected two compounding errors: incorrect text features and biased layer selection. Both are fully resolved in the final analysis.
371	
372	\paragraph{Commitment to transparency.} We report this bug to maintain scientific integrity. All code, including the git history showing the bug and its fix, will be released with the camera-ready version. Researchers reproducing our results can verify the correction independently.
373	
374	\section{Adaptive Two-Stage Detection}
375	\label{app:two_stage}
376	
377	We explored whether a two-stage adaptive window strategy could improve the precision-coverage tradeoff beyond fixed-window detection. The approach uses a short screening window ($W_{\text{screen}}$) to flag candidate unsafe segments, followed by a longer confirmation window ($W_{\text{confirm}}$) applied only to flagged segments. A trace is classified as unsafe if at least $k$ steps exceed the threshold during the confirmation stage.
378	
379	We evaluated all combinations of $W_{\text{screen}} \in \{1, 3, 5\}$, $W_{\text{confirm}} \in \{10, 15, 20, 25\}$, and $k \in \{1, 3, 5\}$ on R1-8B test traces ($n{=}55$; 39 jailbreak, 16 benign). \cref{fig:pareto_two_stage} shows the precision-coverage Pareto front for both methods. The best two-stage configuration ($W_{\text{screen}}{=}3$, $W_{\text{confirm}}{=}15$, $k{=}1$) achieves 72.2\% precision at 76.9\% coverage, compared to 70.9\% precision at 76.9\% coverage for single-stage $W{=}15$. This +1.3pp precision gain at matched coverage represents a Pareto improvement, but the magnitude is marginal. At higher confirmation thresholds ($k{=}3$, $k{=}5$), two-stage detection t

356	The best BOCPD configuration ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 93.0\% detection rate with +3.45 steps lead. The simple threshold baseline achieves 65.1\% detection with superior lead (+4.89 steps). All CUSUM configurations underperform the simple threshold on lead time.
357	
358	These results reveal a coverage-timing tradeoff. BOCPD ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 28pp higher detection rate than simple thresholding (93.0\% vs.\ 65.1\%), but with lower lead (+3.45 vs.\ +4.89 steps). The transition toward unsafe reasoning is gradual rather than abrupt, so change-point methods cannot exploit discontinuities for early warning. When missed detections are costly, BOCPD's coverage advantage justifies its use despite the timing penalty. When early intervention is the priority, simple thresholding provides better lead time.
359	
360	
361	\section{M002 Bug Disclosure}

_source line 3417 · 2026-05-17T17:57:34.743085+00:00_

4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, all 12 tests pass. The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency risks the same systematic bias toward early layers. This bias is not specific to safety probing. Any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two independent layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect layer selection methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.

_source line 3423 · 2026-05-17T17:57:57.078670+00:00_

9	We present the first systematic evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two parameter scales (7--8B, 32B). Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A natural composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because early layers produce more volatile representations that cross detection thresholds more frequently, inflating the crossing rate component independently of true discriminative power. At these biased layers, only 5 of 12 statistical tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent ``scale-dependent'' HS advantage that is significant at 7--8B but absent at 32B. A five-metric ablation reveals that four complementary metrics all converge on deep layers for every model (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these corrected layers, all 12 tests pass Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction ($\alpha{=}0.05$), with HS precision exceeding text by +15.4 to +23.6 percentage points (pp) across all four models (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). The layer selection metric, not scale or architecture, is the true moderator of probe performance. This result has implications beyond our specific task: any study that uses composite metrics to select probe layers risks the same systematic bias, and sensitivity analysis across multiple selection criteria should be standard practice in the probing literature.

100:\paragraph{Detection criterion.} We use $K{=}5$ (five consecutive threshold crossings required for detection) throughout all experiments. This criterion does not affect step-level metrics (balanced accuracy, precision, FPR), which are computed over all steps independently. It governs coverage and lead time: a trace is ``detected'' only when five consecutive steps exceed the threshold, reducing spurious detections from isolated high-scoring steps.

_source line 3427 · 2026-05-17T17:58:08.036827+00:00_

52	\paragraph{Mechanistic explanation.} Shallow transformer layers produce more volatile representations across reasoning steps. This volatility causes probe scores derived from shallow layers to cross any fixed threshold more frequently, inflating the crossing rate component independently of whether the probe has learned genuinely informative features. The product metric therefore favors layers that are ``trigger-happy'' (high crossing rate due to representational instability) rather than layers whose representations encode the semantic distinction between pre-CP and post-CP states. The effect is strongest in deeper models: in 64-layer models (R1-32B, QwQ-32B), the biased metric selects layers at 3--16\% depth, while principled metrics converge on layers at 94--98\% depth.

_source line 3429 · 2026-05-17T17:58:14.056787+00:00_

68	\paragraph{Why deep layers are correct.} Our claim that deep layers provide the appropriate selection does not rest on the circular argument that ``HS probes outperform text probes at deep layers.'' Three independent lines of evidence support this conclusion. First, the mechanistic analysis above identifies a specific, quantifiable bias in the crossing-rate metric that artificially inflates shallow-layer scores. Second, four of five selection metrics, each capturing a different aspect of probe quality, converge on deep layers. Third, the probing literature consistently finds that task-relevant semantic representations form in mid-to-deep transformer layers~\citep{belinkov2022probing, tenney2019bert}. The convergence of mechanistic reasoning, empirical metric agreement, and established probing theory provides a non-circular justification for deep-layer selection.

60	
61	\paragraph{32B models.} QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, L60, $W{=}3$) shows +15.4pp balanced accuracy ($p{<}0.001$), +23.6pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$9.8pp FPR ($p{=}0.004$), all surviving correction. R1-32B ($n{=}20$, L63, $W{=}1$) shows +18.7pp balanced accuracy ($p{<}0.001$), +15.4pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$14.6pp FPR ($p{=}0.007$), all surviving correction. The precision advantage ranges from +15.4 to +23.6pp across all four models.
62	
63	\paragraph{Control task selectivity.} Following \citet{hewitt2019control}, selectivity scores confirm genuine safety signal beyond positional artifacts: R1-8B 12.5pp, OT-7B 18.3pp, QwQ-32B 15.3pp, R1-32B 7.5pp. All four models exceed the 5pp threshold.
64	
65	\paragraph{Caveats.} Three qualifications apply. First, the validation-selected $W$ differs per model ($W{=}15$ for R1-8B, $W{=}3$ for OT-7B and QwQ-32B, $W{=}1$ for R1-32B). The tests measure whether HS outperforms text at each model's best operating point, not at a uniform window. Second, R1-32B at $W{=}1$ achieves only 25\% coverage (5 of 20 test traces detected), which limits practical utility despite strong per-detection performance. At $W{\geq}3$, R1-32B significance vanishes due to the dimensionality constraint ($51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ training traces). Third, QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps) compared to text probes (3.7 to 12.3 steps): HS probes are more accurate but detect later on this model. In safety deployment, earlier detection by several steps may outweigh higher per-step accuracy. Fourth, on benign traces that contain no commitment point, all probing configurations produce trace-level false alarm rates between 39\% and 62\% across the four models [TODO: verify per-model breakdown from artifacts].
66

## 需要更新的数字

旧数字：QwQ-32B 15.3pp (L2), R1-32B 7.5pp (L10)
新数字：QwQ-32B **14.4pp** (L60), R1-32B **19.7pp** (L63)

R1-8B 12.5pp (L14) 和 OT-7B 18.3pp (L16) 不变（biased = corrected layer）。

### 1. experiments.tex — selectivity 行（§4.3 附近）
当前："R1-8B 12.5pp, OT-7B 18.3pp, QwQ-32B 15.3pp, R1-32B 7.5pp"
改为："R1-8B 12.5pp, OT-7B 18.3pp, QwQ-32B 14.4pp, R1-32B 19.7pp"

### 2. abstract.tex — selectivity range
当前："selectivity 7.5--18.3pp above chance"
改为："selectivity 12.5--19.7pp above chance"

### 3. appendix.tex §app:control — Results 段
更新 selectivity 数字为 corrected layer 值：
- "QwQ-32B 15.3pp (layer 2)" → "QwQ-32B 14.4pp (layer 60)"
- "R1-32B 7.5pp (layer 10)" → "R1-32B 19.7pp (layer 63)"
- 删除 "camera-ready" 计划语句（数据已有）
- 更新 "R1-32B's weaker selectivity (7.5pp, near the threshold)" → "R1-32B achieves the strongest selectivity (19.7pp) at the corrected layer, a 2.6$\times$ increase from 7.5pp at the biased layer L10"
- 删除或更新 "QwQ position baseline (70.6%) > HS probe (67.8%)" 的 L2 数据，替换为 L60 数据：position baseline 0.705 vs HS 0.667

### 4. appendix.tex §app:control — Layer provenance note 更新
删除推测性语言（"selectivity at corrected layers would equal or exceed"），替换为实际数据：
"QwQ-32B selectivity is 14.4pp at L60 (comparable to 15.3pp at L2), confirming that safety-specific signal persists at deep layers. R1-32B selectivity increases from 7.5pp at L10 to 19.7pp at L63, demonstrating that the corrected layer captures substantially more safety-relevant structure. The position baseline at L60 (0.705) still slightly exceeds the HS probe (0.667) for QwQ-32B, consistent with the main-text finding that QwQ's HS advantage is concentrated in precision and FPR rather than balanced accuracy."

### 5. discussion.tex — 新增一句
在 "Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom" paragraph 尾部加：

_source line 3465 · 2026-05-17T18:03:17.609634+00:00_

288	\section{Control Task Methodology}
289	\label{app:control}
290	
291	We follow the selectivity framework of \cite{hewitt2019control} to verify that our probes capture genuine safety-relevant structure rather than surface-level correlations.
292	
293	\paragraph{Procedure.} For each model, we randomly permute the binary labels (pre-CP vs. post-CP) across all steps while preserving the marginal label distribution. We then retrain the probe with identical architecture (L2-regularized logistic regression) and hyperparameters on these shuffled labels. Selectivity is defined as:
294	\begin{equation}
295	    \text{Selectivity} = \text{Acc}_{\text{linguistic}} - \text{Acc}_{\text{control}}
296	\end{equation}
297	where $\text{Acc}_{\text{linguistic}}$ is the probe accuracy on true labels and $\text{Acc}_{\text{control}}$ is the accuracy on permuted labels. A selectivity of zero indicates that the probe exploits only non-linguistic features (e.g., position); higher values confirm that the representation encodes task-relevant information.
298	
299	\paragraph{Results.} We repeat the control experiment 5 times (10 for OT-7B) with different random permutations and report the mean control accuracy. Selectivity scores are: OT-7B 18.3pp (layer 16), QwQ-32B 15.3pp (layer 2), R1-8B 12.5pp (layer 14), R1-32B 7.5pp (layer 10). All values exceed the 5pp threshold recommended by \cite{hewitt2019control}. OT-7B achieves the highest selectivity at a mid-depth transformer layer (57\%), consistent with R1-8B's pattern at 44\% depth. R1-32B's weaker selectivity (7.5pp, near the threshold) is consistent with its overall weaker hidden-state signal. On QwQ-32B, the position baseline accuracy (70.6\%) slightly exceeds the HS probe (67.8\%), indicating that layer 2 encodes strong positional information alongside safety-relevant structure.
300	
301	\paragraph{Layer provenance note.} QwQ-32B (15.3pp) and R1-32B (7.5pp) selectivity scores were computed at the originally selected layers (L2 and L10)

_source line 3471 · 2026-05-17T18:03:32.568887+00:00_

63	\paragraph{Control task selectivity.} Following \citet{hewitt2019control}, selectivity scores confirm genuine safety signal beyond positional artifacts: R1-8B 12.5pp, OT-7B 18.3pp, QwQ-32B 15.3pp, R1-32B 7.5pp. All four models exceed the 5pp threshold.
64	
65	\paragraph{Caveats.} Three qualifications apply. First, the validation-selected $W$ differs per model ($W{=}15$ for R1-8B, $W{=}3$ for OT-7B and QwQ-32B, $W{=}1$ for R1-32B). The tests measure whether HS outperforms text at each model's best operating point, not at a uniform window. Second, R1-32B at $W{=}1$ achieves only 25\% coverage (5 of 20 test traces detected), which limits practical utility despite strong per-detection performance. At $W{\geq}3$, R1-32B significance vanishes due to the dimensionality constraint ($51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ training traces). Third, QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps) compared to text probes (3.7 to 12.3 steps): HS probes are more accurate but detect later on this model. In safety deployment, earlier detection by several steps may outweigh higher per-step accuracy. Fourth, on benign traces that contain no commitment point, all probing configurations produce trace-level false alarm rates between 39\% and 62\% across the four models [TODO: verify per-model breakdown from artifacts].

_source line 3473 · 2026-05-17T18:03:33.372986+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this \emph{safety commitment} in real time is essential for monitoring systems, yet no prior work has systematically compared hidden-state (HS) probes against text-based monitors for this task. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal probing for safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our primary methodological finding is that layer selection methodology can reverse a study's conclusions: the composite metric \texttt{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15 to +24 percentage points (pp) across all four models ($n{=}19$--$47$ test traces; $n{=}19$--$20$ at 32B scale). Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities, providing the most robust single default for safety monitoring. Seven alternative approaches (change-point detection, geometric distance, Representation Engineering, CCS, nonlinear probes, cross-model transfer via PCA projection and direct application) all fail, establishing clear boundaries of current methods. Control tasks confirm genuine safety-specific structure (selectivity 7.5--18.3pp above chance).
2

_source line 3475 · 2026-05-17T18:03:34.779709+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, all 12 tests pass. The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency risks the same systematic bias toward early layers. This bias is not specific to safety probing. Any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect layer selection methodology rather than the phenomenon under study.
5

[Director 指令] [Patch — MF4 Citation Integration] 当前有效引用 20 篇，目标 ≥30。bib 条目已存在，需要在 Related Work 正文中自然引用。

## 必须引用的 6 篇新论文

在 related_work.tex 各对应段落中添加引用，每篇 1-2 句定位与本文关系：

### 1. zhou2024alignment → §Safety in Reasoning Models 或 §Hidden-State Safety Detection
Zhou et al. (2024) 做 layer-level safety analysis（hidden states 中的 alignment/jailbreak 信号）。与本文关系：他们分析 standard LLMs 的 layer-wise safety representation，我们扩展到 reasoning models 的 temporal commitment detection + 发现 layer selection bias。
引用句示例："Recent work has begun examining how safety-relevant information distributes across transformer layers~\citep{zhou2024alignment}, but these analyses target standard instruction-tuned models rather than the temporal commitment process in reasoning models."

### 2. zhao2025harmfulness → §Hidden-State Safety Detection 或新建 §Safety Representation 段
Zhao et al. (2025) 发现 harmfulness 和 refusal 在 hidden states 中有独立方向。与 arditi2024refusal（single refusal direction）互补。
引用句示例："The internal representation of safety is multi-dimensional: \citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction that mediates model safety behavior, while \citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show that harmfulness and refusal are encoded in separable directions."

### 3. arditi2024refusal → 同上（与 zhao2025 一起构成 §Safety Representation 小主题）

### 4. hcot2025 → §Safety in Reasoning Models
H-CoT (2025) 做 reasoning chain safety hijacking。与本文关系：他们 characterize reasoning 过程中的 safety failure mode（从外部操纵 CoT），我们 detect reasoning model 自发的 safety commitment transition。
引用句示例："H-CoT~\citep{hcot2025} demonstrates that reasoning chains can be externally hijacked to bypass safety constraints, complementing the self-jailbreak phenomenon where harmful transitions originate from the model's own deliberation."

### 5. safedream2026 → §Hidden-State Safety Detection
SafeDream (2026) 做 proactive early jailbreak detection（safety world model）。与本文关系：他们用 predictive model 做 proactive detection，我们用 passive probing。
引用句示例："SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026} proposes a proactive world model for early jailbreak detection, in contrast to our passive probing approach that characterizes the detection frontier without active intervention."

### 6. hu2026refusal_dynamics → §Safety in Reasoning Models（⚠️ novelty threat，需要差异化）
"Tracing the Dynamics of Refusal" (2026) 可能与本文有高 overlap。差异化要点（根据标题推断）：
- 他们可能做 refusal dynamics（standard refusal pathway），我们做 safety commitment in self-jailbreak（reasoning model 特有的 commitment point detection）
- 我们的 primary contribution 是 layer selection sensitivity（methodological），他们大概率不做 5-metric ablation
- 我们有 comprehensive negative results（7 methods fail）
引用句示例："Concurrent work traces refusal dynamics in language models~\citep{hu2026refusal_dynamics}; our work differs in focusing on the self-jailbreak commitment process specific to reasoning models, with an emphasis on the methodological finding that layer selection metrics can reverse probe-based conclusions."

### 7. ahrend2026safer_traces → §Safety in Reasoning Models
"Safer Reasoning Traces" (2026)。
引用句示例："Approaches to improving reasoning trace safety include \citet{ahrend2026safer_traces}, who propose methods for generating safer reasoning traces; our work instead focuses on detecting when existing traces commit to harmful output."

### 9. wang2025lrmsafety → §Safety in Reasoning Models（broad survey cite）
引用句示例："For a comprehensive survey of safety challenges in large reasoning models, see \citet{wang2025lrmsafety}."

### 10. alain2017understanding → §Probing Methodology and Layer Selection

### 11. conneau2018probing → §Probing Methodology and Layer Selection

_source line 3497 · 2026-05-17T18:04:59.520319+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification. \citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. Both operate on generated text and assume that safety violations are linguistically explicit in the CoT. These works collectively characterize the problem or propose text-level interventions; none conducts a systematic modality comparison to determine whether hidden states or text better supports commitment detection.
5	
6	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
7	A parallel line of work uses internal model activations to detect safety violations. TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial mani

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification. \\citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \\citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. Both operate on generated text and assume that safety violations are linguistically explicit in the CoT. These works collectively characterize the problem or propose text-level interventions; none conducts a systematic modality comparison to determine whether hidden states or text better supports commitment detection.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification~\\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}. \\citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \\citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. H-CoT~\\cite{hcot2025} demonstrates that reasoning chains can be externally hijacked to bypass safety constraints, complementing the self-jailbreak phenomenon where harmful transitions originate from the model's own deliberation. The reliability of reasoning traces is further complicated by unfaithful reasoning~\\cite{chen2025unfaithful}, where models' stated reasoning does not reflect their actual decision process, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text entirely. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. \\citet{ahrend2026safer_traces} measure and mitigate information leakage in reasoning traces, addressing a complementary safety dimension. Concurrent work traces refusal dynamics in standard language models~\\cite{hu2026refusal_dynamics}; our work differs in focusing on the self-jailbreak commitment process specific to reasoning models, with an emphasis on the methodological finding that layer selection metrics can reverse probe-based conclusions. These works collectively characterize the problem or propose text-level interventions; none conducts a systematic modality comparison to determine whether hidden states or text better supports commitment detection."
}
```

    "new_string": "\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification~\\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}. \\citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \\citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. H-CoT~\\cite{hcot2025} demonstrates that reasoning chains can be externally hijacked to bypass safety constraints, complementing the self-jailbreak phenomenon where harmful transitions originate from the model's own deliberation. The reliability of reasoning traces is further complicated by unfaithful reasoning~\\cite{chen2025unfaithful}, where models' stated reasoning does not reflect their actual decision process, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text entirely. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. \\citet{ahrend2026safer_traces} measure and mitigate information leakage in reasoning traces, addressing a complementary safety dimension. Concurrent work traces refusal dynamics in standard language models~\\cite{hu2026refusal_dynamics}; our work differs in focusing on the self-jailbreak commitment process specific to reasoning models, with an emphasis on the methodological finding that layer selection metrics can reverse probe-based conclusions. <details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nA parallel line of work uses internal model activations to detect safety violations. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial manipulation of the model's input (jailbreak prompts, adversarial suffixes). They operate at the token level and were evaluated against external attack vectors on instruction-tuned models that lack extended reasoning. The self-jailbreak setting differs fundamentally because the harmful transition is internal to the model's own deliberation rather than externally induced, and the relevant signal spans multiple reasoning steps rather than individual tokens. We reproduce TrajGuard in our setting (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find that its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal for safety commitment resides in minor PCA components rather than the principal directions that dominate Mahalanobis distance computation.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nA parallel line of work uses internal model activations to detect safety violations. \\citet{zhou2024alignment} show that safety-relevant information distributes across transformer layers in standard LLMs, with early layers already distinguishing malicious from benign inputs. The internal representation of safety is multi-dimensional: \\citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction that mediates model safety behavior, while \\citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show that harmfulness and refusal are encoded in separable directions, suggesting that text-level monitors may capture only the refusal signal while missing the harmfulness encoding. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. SafeDream~\\cite{safedream2026} proposes a proactive world model for early jailbreak detection, in contrast to passive probing approaches. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial manipulation of the model's input (jailbreak prompts, adversarial suffixes). They operate at the token level and were evaluated against external attack vectors on instruction-tuned models that lack extended reasoning. The self-jailbreak setting differs fundamentally because the harmful transition is internal to the model's own deliberation rather than externally induced, and the relevant signal spans multiple reasoning steps rather than individual tokens. We reproduce TrajGuard in our setting (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find that its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal for safety commitment resides in minor PCA components rather than the principal directions that dominate Mahalanobis distance computation."
}
```
