TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial manipulation of the model's input (jailbreak prompts, adversarial suffixes). They operate at the token level and were evaluated against external attack vectors on instruction-tuned models that lack extended reasoning. The self-jailbreak setting differs fundamentally because the harmful transition is internal to the model's own deliberation rather than externally induced, and the relevant signal spans multiple reasoning steps rather than individual tokens. We reproduce TrajGuard in our setting (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find that its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal for safety commitment resides in minor PCA components rather than the principal directions that dominate Mahalanobis distance computation.",
    "new_string": "\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nA parallel line of work uses internal model activations to detect safety violations. \\citet{zhou2024alignment} show that safety-relevant information distributes across transformer layers in standard LLMs, with early layers already distinguishing malicious from benign inputs. The internal representation of safety is multi-dimensional: \\citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction that mediates model safety behavior, while \\citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show that harmfulness and refusal are encoded in separable directions, suggesting that text-level monitors may capture only the refusal signal while missing the harmfulness encoding. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} computes Mahalanobis distance from safe activation trajectories and achieves above 95\\% defense rates on standard instruction-tuned LLMs. SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers that trigger safety interventions when activation patterns indicate harmful intent. SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation and detects harmful content before it reaches users. SafeDream~\\cite{safedream2026} proposes a proactive world model for early jailbreak detection, in contrast to passive probing approaches. These methods share a critical assumption: the threat originates from adversarial manipulation of the model's input (jailbreak prompts, adversarial suffixes). They operate at the token level and were evaluated against external attack vectors on instruction-tuned models that lack extended reasoning. The self-jailbreak setting differs fundamentally because the harmful transition is internal to the model's own deliberation rather than externally induced, and the relevant signal spans multiple reasoning steps rather than individual tokens. We reproduce TrajGuard in our setting (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find that its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal for safety commitment resides in minor PCA components rather than the principal directions that dominate Mahalanobis distance computation."
  }
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}\nLinear probing is the standard tool for investigating what information transformer representations encode~\\cite{belinkov2019analysis,hewitt2019control}. \\citet{belinkov2019analysis} survey probing methods across NLP tasks and establish that task-specific representations form in different layers depending on the linguistic level of the target property. \\citet{tenney2019bert} conduct layer-by-layer analysis showing that syntactic information concentrates in middle layers while semantic features emerge in deeper layers. \\citet{hewitt2019control} introduce control tasks and selectivity metrics to distinguish genuine representational structure from probe memorization, a methodology we adopt to validate our safety probes (\\cref{app:control}). Despite this methodological foundation, layer selection remains an underexamined researcher degree of freedom. Most probing studies select layers by a single validation metric and report results at that layer without sensitivity analysis. We demonstrate that this practice can reverse a study's central conclusion: a composite metric (balanced accuracy multiplied by crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models, producing qualitatively different findings than four alternative metrics that converge on deep layers (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}\nLinear probing has a long history in representation analysis, from early layer-wise classifiers~\\cite{alain2017understanding} to systematic probing of sentence representations~\\cite{conneau2018probing}, establishing the methodological foundation for investigating what information transformer layers encode~\\cite{belinkov2019analysis,hewitt2019control}. \\citet{belinkov2019analysis} survey probing methods across NLP tasks and establish that task-specific representations form in different layers depending on the linguistic level of the target property. \\citet{tenney2019bert} conduct layer-by-layer analysis showing that syntactic information concentrates in middle layers while semantic features emerge in deeper layers. \\citet{hewitt2019control} introduce control tasks and selectivity metrics to distinguish genuine representational structure from probe memorization, a methodology we adopt to validate our safety probes (\\cref{app:control}). \\citet{belinkov2022probing} provide a critical assessment of probing methodology, noting that probe complexity can confound representational conclusions. Despite this methodological foundation, layer selection remains an underexamined researcher degree of freedom. Most probing studies select layers by a single validation metric and report results at that layer without sensitivity analysis. We demonstrate that this practice can reverse a study's central conclusion: a composite metric (balanced accuracy multiplied by crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models, producing qualitatively different findings than four alternative metrics that converge on deep layers (\\cref{sec:layer-results})."
}
```

_source line 3555 · 2026-05-17T19:36:17.878328+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] Round 5 审稿修复。EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25 deadline。引用不需要验证（Agent 已 WebSearch 验证）。

以下是全部需要修改的内容，按优先级排列：

## 1. PCA-384d 维度控制段落 [N1 CRITICAL]

在 experiments.tex 的 Section 5.1 (HS vs Text) 末尾、Caveats 段前，新增一段 "Dimensionality control"：

核心数据：
- PCA 降维 HS 到 384d（与 text encoder 维度精确匹配）
- Precision: 4/4 显著（R1-8B +23.1pp p<0.001, OT-7B +16.8pp p<0.001, QwQ +18.7pp p<0.001, R1-32B +18.4pp p=0.002）
- 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests pass（2 non-sig: R1-8B bal_acc p=0.731, QwQ FPR adjusted p too strict）
- PCA explained variance: 79-92% across models
- 结论：HS precision advantage is modality effect, not dimensionality artifact

参考措辞（Reviewer 建议）：
"To rule out a purely dimensional explanation (HS features are 4096-5120d vs text embeddings at 384d), we apply PCA to reduce HS features to 384 dimensions and repeat the bootstrap comparison. At matched dimensionality, HS precision advantage persists on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all p<0.012), with 10 of 12 tests passing Holm-Bonferroni correction. Balanced accuracy shows weaker effects (1/4 non-significant), consistent with discriminative signal residing partly in minor principal components. The safety-critical precision metric is robust to dimensionality reduction."

## 2. "12/12" 呈现方式修改 [N2 CRITICAL]

全文将 "all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests" 的呈现方式从强调 "12 independent confirmations" 改为强调 "4 independent models"。

修改位置：Abstract, Introduction (Contribution 2), Experiments Section 5.1。

核心 reframe：
- 旧："all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority"
- 新："HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction, 12 tests, α=0.05)"

在 Method Section 3.5 (Evaluation Protocol) 的 Statistical testing 段落末尾加一句：
"Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction (precision and FPR share false positives). Under positive dependence, the Holm-Bonferroni procedure becomes more conservative, meaning our correction is stricter than under independence."

## 3. QwQ timing qualifier [N3 CRITICAL]

Abstract 中在 precision gains 句后加限定：加上 "with model-specific detection timing trade-offs at 32B scale"。

Introduction Contribution 2 已有 model-specific caveats，确认 QwQ 的 "higher precision at cost of later detection" 已明确体现。如果不够明确，补一句。

## 4. "All fail" → nuanced [W2]

全文搜索 "fail" 相关表述，修改为 nuanced framing：
- "Seven alternative detection approaches all fail" → "Seven alternative approaches produce inconsistent or limited results"
- 包括：Abstract Contribution 3, Introduction Contribution 3, Experiments Section 5.5 opening
- Contribution 3 中 "all fail" 具体替换为 "produce inconsistent or limited results, defining method boundaries"

在 Section 5.5 (Negative Results) 中为 RepE-QwQ 添加 nuanced 讨论：
"RepE approaches supervised balanced accuracy on QwQ-32B (0.756 vs 0.740) but with substantially worse precision (0.51 vs 0.70) and FPR (0.31 vs 0.12), indicating that a single mean-difference direction captures overall discrimination but not the precision needed for safety monitoring."

## 5. Conclusion 进一步压缩 [W4]

检查 Conclusion 与 Discussion 是否有逐句对应的重复。如果有，删除 Conclusion 中的重复部分，保留最多 3-4 句核心 takeaway。

## 6. 泛化 claim → hypothesis [W5]

Discussion 中：
- "any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate..." → "we hypothesize that any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate..."
- 加一句："Validation on other probing tasks (sentiment, factuality, toxicity) is needed to confirm generality."

## 7. "四指标收敛" → "两个 metric families" [W6]

Method Section 3.3 和 Discussion 中：
- "four complementary metrics converge" → "metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers"
- 加 AUROC 发现到 Discussion 的 layer selection 段落：
  "Even among unbiased metrics, the choice between threshold-based and threshold-free criteria affects layer selection: AUROC peaks at mid-depth layers (~50%) while precision peaks at deep layers (>90%), further demonstrating that layer selection methodology is a critical researcher degree of freedom."

## 8. 引用已由 Agent 完成

Agent 已添加 7 篇新引用到 references.bib 和 related_work.tex。请检查 positioning 是否自然、无 AI 写作痕迹。如有需要微调。当前 bib 38 条。

## 9. Minor fixes

- experiments.tex Caveats 段："Fourth" 编号前面是否有 "First, Second, Third"？如果不连贯，修复编号。
- 检查全文无 em-dash、解释性 en-dash、-ing phrase endings。

## 写作规范提醒
- 无 AI 写作痕迹（零 em-dash、零 en-dash 解释性用法、无 -ing phrase 结尾）
- 新增段落不超过 5-8 句
- 不要 "Furthermore" / "Moreover" / "Additionally" 连续使用
- 数字精度与全文一致（pp 保留一位小数）

59	\paragraph{7--8B models.} R1-8B ($n{=}43$, L14, $W{=}15$) shows HS advantages of +8.2pp balanced accuracy ($p{=}0.005$), +22.4pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$7.4pp FPR ($p{=}0.044$). All three survive Holm-Bonferroni correction. OT-7B ($n{=}47$, L16, matched $W{=}3$) shows +10.7pp balanced accuracy ($p{<}0.001$), +21.9pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$17.1pp FPR ($p{<}0.001$), with all three tests surviving correction. The precision advantage is consistent across these architecturally distinct models: +22.4pp on the Llama-based R1-8B and +21.9pp on the Qwen-based OT-7B, despite different training methods (R1 distillation vs.\ STILL).

61	\paragraph{32B models.} QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$--$20$, L60, $W{=}3$) shows +15.4pp balanced accuracy ($p{<}0.001$), +23.6pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$9.8pp FPR ($p{=}0.004$), all surviving correction. R1-32B ($n{=}20$, L63, $W{=}1$) shows +18.7pp balanced accuracy ($p{<}0.001$), +15.4pp precision ($p{<}0.001$), and $-$14.6pp FPR ($p{=}0.007$), all surviving correction. The precision advantage ranges from +15.4 to +23.6pp across all four models.
62	
63	\paragraph{Control task selectivity.} Following \citet{hewitt2019control}, selectivity scores confirm genuine safety signal beyond positional artifacts: R1-8B 12.5pp, OT-7B 18.3pp, QwQ-32B 14.4pp, R1-32B 19.7pp. All four models exceed the 5pp threshold.

41	\paragraph{GRU sequence models.} We train single-layer GRU classifiers that process the full step sequence to produce per-step predictions. We evaluate two variants: a single-layer variant (using hidden states from the best-performing layer) and a multi-layer variant (concatenating representations from 7 layers spanning the model depth). Architectural details are in \cref{app:gru_details}.
42	
43	\paragraph{Training procedure.} All probes are trained with Adam (learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, $\beta_1{=}0.9$, $\beta_2{=}0.999$), batch size 16, maximum 50 epochs with early stopping (patience 10 on validation balanced accuracy). Inputs are z-score normalized per dimension using training set statistics.
44	
45	\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity Analysis}
46	\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}
47	
48	Hidden-state probing requires selecting which transformer layer to extract representations from. This choice constitutes a researcher degree of freedom that, as we demonstrate, can entirely determine whether hidden-state probes appear to outperform text-based alternatives. We conduct a systematic sensitivity analysis to expose this bias and recommend principled selection criteria.
49	
50	\paragraph{The composite metric bias.} A natural approach is to select the layer that maximizes some composite performance metric on the validation set. The default composite metric used in our initial experiments, the product of balanced accuracy and crossing rate (the fraction of traces where the probe score crosses the detection threshold at least once), is a natural choice for threshold-based detectors that must balance discrimination with coverage. We show that this specific composite systematically biases toward shallow layers through a mechanism unrelated to discriminative power.
51

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT), producing dozens of internal reasoning steps before generating a final response. This deliberative process interleaves safety-aware reasoning with content generation across many steps, and the very capacity for self-reflection introduces a failure mode absent from standard instruction-tuned models. Recent analyses have documented that reasoning models can ``self-jailbreak'': without any adversarial modification to the input prompt, the model's own CoT transitions from considering safety constraints to committing to produce harmful content~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. In a typical self-jailbreak trace, the model begins by acknowledging that a request may be harmful, engages in several steps of safety deliberation, and then gradually shifts toward compliance before abandoning safety reasoning entirely. We refer to the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). Before the CP, the model is still weighing refusal; after it, harmful generation proceeds unchecked. This failure mode is qualitatively different from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs: the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning rather than from a crafted input. Detecting this transition in real time is essential for monitoring systems that must intervene before harmful content reaches users, yet no existing detector was designed for this internal commitment process.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target a fundamentally different problem. Hidden-state (HS) probing methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} monitor internal activations to classify adversarial inputs directed at instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs), achieving above 95\% defense rates on standard b
…[truncated 6277 chars]

_source line 3565 · 2026-05-17T19:36:52.797182+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work provides the first systematic cross-model evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures, two training methods, and two scales.
4	The central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion: a composite metric incorporating crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage, while four complementary metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\% depth).
5	At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected tests confirm significant HS superiority (precision +15.4 to +23.6pp), and seven alternative methods fail, though practical detection profiles diverge by model (R1-32B provides early warning at narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides broad significance with poor timing).
6	Closing the 85.5\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and the theoretical detection frontier will require causal intervention techniques such as activation patching, extension to multilingual and multi-turn settings, evaluation at intermediate scales (13B, 70B), and exploration of stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond the PCA-based and direct transfer approaches tested here.
7

_source line 3567 · 2026-05-17T19:36:59.073765+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this \emph{safety commitment} in real time is essential for monitoring systems, yet no prior work has systematically compared hidden-state (HS) probes against text-based monitors for this task. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal probing for safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our primary methodological finding is that layer selection methodology can reverse a study's conclusions: the composite metric \texttt{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage. At correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected hypothesis tests confirm significant HS superiority, with precision gains of +15 to +24 percentage points (pp) across all four models ($n{=}19$--$47$ test traces; $n{=}19$--$20$ at 32B scale). Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities, providing the most robust single default for safety monitoring. Seven alternative approaches (change-point detection, geometric distance, Representation Engineering, CCS, nonlinear probes, cross-model transfer via PCA projection and direct application) all fail, establishing clear boundaries of current methods. Control tasks confirm genuine safety-specific structure (selectivity 12.5--19.7pp above chance).
2

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, all 12 tests pass. The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency is susceptible to the same bias mechanism, whose practical impact depends on the specific task and model depth. This bias is not specific to safety probing. Any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. The cost is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative is that conclusions about representational structure reflect layer selection methodology rather than the phenomenon under study. The control task provides independent corroboration: R1-32B selectivity increases from 7.5pp at the biased layer (L10) to 19.7pp at the corrected layer (L63), confirming that the biased metric selected layers where positional features dominate over genuine safety structure.
5

  "old_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Consistent HS precision advantage at correctly selected layers.} At deep layers selected by unbiased metrics, HS probes significantly outperform text probes (against a 384-dimensional sentence encoder, all-MiniLM-L6-v2, as the text baseline) on all four models (12/12 Holm-Bonferroni at $\\alpha{=}0.05$, precision +15.4 to +23.6pp; \\cref{sec:hs-text}, \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Model-specific trade-offs at 32B qualify the practical implications: R1-32B provides excellent early detection (+13 steps lead time) but narrow coverage at the significant temporal window, while QwQ shows robust significance across all 15 window sizes but later detection than text (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).",
  "new_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Consistent HS precision advantage at correctly selected layers.} At deep layers selected by unbiased metrics, HS probes significantly outperform text probes (against a 384-dimensional sentence encoder, all-MiniLM-L6-v2, as the text baseline) on all four models across all three metrics (Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction, 12 tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp; \\cref{sec:hs-text}, \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}).   "old_string": "\\paragraph{Statistical testing.} All modality comparisons (HS vs.\\ text probes) use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap test (1000 resamples) on trace-level metric values. For each resample, we draw paired differences with replacement, center the distribution at the null hypothesis of zero difference, and compute the two-tailed exceedance probability. We conduct 12 hypothesis tests (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models) and apply the Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. This procedure controls the family-wise error rate while maintaining power across the test battery.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Statistical testing.} All modality comparisons (HS vs.\\ text probes) use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap test (1000 resamples) on trace-level metric values. For each resample, we draw paired differences with replacement, center the distribution at the null hypothesis of zero difference, and compute the two-tailed exceedance probability. We conduct 12 hypothesis tests (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models) and apply the Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. This procedure controls the family-wise error rate while maintaining power across the test battery. Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction (precision and FPR share false positives). Under positive dependence, the Holm-Bonferroni procedure becomes more conservative, meaning our correction is stricter than under independence."
}
```

We conduct 12 hypothesis tests (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models) and apply the Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. This procedure controls the family-wise error rate while maintaining power across the test battery. Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction (precision and FPR share false positives). 111	The complementary profiles suggest that the practical value of HS monitoring depends on model-specific detection requirements. Early warning applications favor R1-32B's high lead time despite narrow coverage. Broad reliable detection applications favor QwQ-32B's robust significance despite poor timing. These divergent patterns within the same architecture underscore that per-model validation is essential.
112	
113	\subsection{Negative Results}\label{sec:negative}
114	
115	\cref{tab:negative} summarizes seven alternative detection approaches that fail to match supervised temporal probing. The failures span change-point detection, geometric methods, direction-based probing, nonlinear models, semi-supervised learning, and cross-model transfer.
116	
117	\begin{table}[t]
118	\centering
119	\small
120	\begin{tabular}{p{1.5cm}p{2.9cm}p{2.3cm}}
121	\toprule
122	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Result} & \textbf{Failure Mode} \\
123	\midrule
124	BOCPD & R1-8B 93\% det., lead +3.5 $<$ thresh.\ +4.9; others 0\% & Commitment gradual \\
125	CUSUM & Worse than threshold, all models & Same as BOCPD \\
126	Mahalanobis & $\Delta d \approx 0$ (R1-8B +0.07, OT-7B +0.03) & Signal in minor PCA dirs. \\
127	RepE & R1-8B 74.9\%, QwQ 75.6\%, R1-32B 55.9\%$^a$ & Model-dependent \\
128	MLP & 3/4 worse than LR & Overfitting ($n$ small) \\
129	CCS$^b$ & R1-8B 77.7\%; R1-32B FPR\,=\,0.41 & No gain over supervised \\

  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work provides the first systematic cross-model evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures, two training methods, and two scales.\nThe central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion: a composite metric incorporating crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage, while four complementary metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\\% depth).\nAt correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected tests confirm significant HS superiority (precision +15.4 to +23.6pp), and seven alternative methods fail, though practical detection profiles diverge by model (R1-32B provides early warning at narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides broad significance with poor timing).\nClosing the 85.5\\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and the theoretical detection frontier will require causal intervention techniques such as activation patching, extension to multilingual and multi-turn settings, evaluation at intermediate scales (13B, 70B), and exploration of stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond the PCA-based and direct transfer approaches tested here.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.\nThe central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers where HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models (Holm-Bonferroni correction, 12 tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp).\nFuture work should close the 85.5\\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer."
}
```

    "old_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work provides the first systematic cross-model evaluation of safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures, two training methods, and two scales.\nThe central methodological finding is that layer selection metric bias can reverse a study's main conclusion: a composite metric incorporating crossing rate systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage, while four complementary metrics converge on deep layers (94--98\\% depth).\nAt correctly selected layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni-corrected tests confirm significant HS superiority (precision +15.4 to +23.6pp), and seven alternative methods fail, though practical detection profiles diverge by model (R1-32B provides early warning at narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides broad significance with poor timing).\nClosing the 85.5\\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and the theoretical detection frontier will require causal intervention techniques such as activation patching, extension to multilingual and multi-turn settings, evaluation at intermediate scales (13B, 70B), and exploration of stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond the PCA-based and direct transfer approaches tested here.",
    "new_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.\nThe central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers where HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models (Holm-Bonferroni correction, 12 tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp).\nFuture work should close the 85.5\\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer."
  }
}
```

_source line 3607 · 2026-05-17T19:40:10.457738+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers where HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models (Holm-Bonferroni correction, 12 tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:118:\paragraph{OT-7B} exhibits a split temporal pattern at layer 16: balanced accuracy peaks at $W{=}3$ (68.8\%) and declines at larger windows ($-$2.9pp by $W{=}25$), while precision and FPR continue improving. Precision rises from 42.8\% ($W{=}1$) to 49.4\% ($W{=}20$), and FPR drops from 12.0\% to 7.5\% over the same range. The split indicates that larger windows reduce false alarms and sharpen positive predictions even as overall classification accuracy degrades. Lead times are shorter than old layer-0 estimates (16.1--39.6 steps) due to the corrected layer selection (\cref{app:ot7b_details}). OT-7B text probes improve monotonically across all metrics (\cref{app:ot7b_details}), so the accuracy saturation is specific to HS probes on this model.

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} introduce a qualitatively new safety failure: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful content generation without any adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}. \citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon and provide step-level annotations of reasoning traces, establishing that commitment to harmful output emerges gradually across multiple reasoning steps. \citet{selfjailbreak} characterize the statistical properties of self-jailbreak, showing that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability, but do not propose a deployable detection mechanism. \citet{hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak} demonstrate that large reasoning models can autonomously jailbreak other LLMs through multi-turn conversations with a 97\% success rate, a finding that identifies reasoning capability itself as a safety threat. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} demonstrates that reasoning chains can be externally hijacked to bypass safety constraints, complementing the self-jailbreak phenomenon where harmful transitions originate from the model's own deliberation. The reliability of reasoning traces is further complicated by unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful}, where models' stated reasoning does not reflect their actual decision process, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text entirely. On the mitigation side, SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning steps toward safety through text-level CoT monitoring, while SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered safety reminders into the reasoning stream. \citet{korbak2025cot_monitorability} argue that chain-of-thought monitorability offers a valuable but fragile safety opportunity, contingent on models continuing to reason in human-readable language. \citet{ahrend2026safe
…[truncated 7247 chars]

  "old_string": "We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection and report comprehensive negative results (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and the related CUSUM statistic fail because safety commitment is gradual rather than abrupt, violating the assumption of a discrete changepoint. CCS provides no gain over supervised probing because the logical consistency objective does not align with temporal commitment structure. RepE mean-difference directions show inconsistent performance at deep layers, failing on R1-32B (0.559 balanced accuracy) while performing comparably to supervised probes on QwQ (0.756), suggesting that the mean-difference direction captures commitment structure in some models but not others. These failures collectively motivate supervised linear probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective detection methodology.",
  "new_string": "We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection and report comprehensive results (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and the related CUSUM statistic produce no early-detection gain because safety commitment is gradual rather than abrupt, violating the assumption of a discrete changepoint. CCS provides no improvement over supervised probing because the logical consistency objective does not align with temporal commitment structure. RepE mean-difference directions show inconsistent performance at deep layers, reaching only 0.559 balanced accuracy on R1-32B while performing comparably to supervised probes on QwQ (0.756), suggesting that the mean-difference direction captures commitment structure in some models but not others. These results collectively motivate supervised linear probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective detection methodology."
}

当前页数分布：Intro 2pp, Related Work 2pp, Methods 3pp, Experiments 4pp, Discussion 1pp, Conclusion <1pp。需要砍 ~5 页。

**压缩策略（按优先级）**：

1. **Experiments (4pp → ~2pp)**：最大压缩空间。
   - 保留：核心 HS vs Text 对比表（12/12 HB 结果）、temporal windowing 主结论、PCA-384d dimensionality control 结果
   - 移入 appendix：per-model detailed breakdown、32B caveats 的详细数据、baseline methods 的逐个分析表、GRU 详细结果
   - 合并：能用一个综合表代替多个小表的就合并

2. **Methods (3pp → ~1.5pp)**：
   - 保留：commitment point 定义、probe architecture（HS/Text/temporal windowing）、evaluation protocol 核心
   - 移入 appendix：5-metric ablation 的完整描述（正文保留结论 + 引用 appendix）、baseline methods 的详细描述（正文一段概述 + appendix 展开）、GRU 架构细节、training procedure 细节
   - Table 1 (models) 保留但可压缩

4. **Introduction (2pp → ~1.5pp)**：

**硬约束**：
- 核心 claims 不能丢：12/12 HB、layer selection reversal、PCA-384d control、temporal windowing
- 移到 appendix 的内容必须在正文有 \cref 引用
- 不改 framing 和结论
- 编译后确认 ≤8 页正文（Limitations + References + Appendix 不计入）

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria. Our experiments address three questions: (1)~Does the layer selection metric determine whether HS probes appear effective? (2)~Do HS probes outperform text probes at correctly selected layers? (3)~Which alternative detection methods produce limited results, and why?
4	
5	\subsection{Experimental Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Models and data.} We study four models spanning two architectures and two parameter scales: R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (Qwen-7B, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level commitment point (CP) annotations. We use a 60/20/20 train/validation/test split across all models (\cref{tab:models}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Training and evaluation.} All probes use L2-regularized logistic regression with balanced class weights. For each model, we select the temporal window $W$ on the validation set from $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$. HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected transformer layer; text probes encode each reasoning step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384 dimensions). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap test (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction across 12 tests (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models, $\alpha{=}0.05$).
10	
11	\paragraph{Layer selection.} We evaluate five layer selection metrics per model (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}). The biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because representational volatility at early layers inflates the crossing rate component. 1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Formally, given a trace of $T$ reasoning steps, the CP is the earliest step $t^*$ such that no step $t > t^*$ contains safety-relevant deliberation. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} (the model is still considering refusal); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} (the model has irrevocably committed to harmful output).
8	
9	We operationalize this definition on HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} traces, which provide step-level annotations of safety reasoning. Each step receives a binary label: pre-CP ($y_t = 0$ for $t < t^*$) or post-CP ($y_t = 1$ for $t \geq t^*$). The detection task is binary classification at each step, producing a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ that estimates the probability that the model has passed its commitment point by step $t$.
10	
11	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama and Qwen) and two parameter scales (7--8B and 32B), summarized in \cref{tab:models}.
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

18	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
19	\midrule
20	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
21	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
22	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\

  "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\\label{sec:setup}\n\nWe study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (Qwen-7B, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence.\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\\label{sec:layer-results}\n\n\\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. Four unbiased metrics (pure balanced accuracy, precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) and L63 (98\\%). Metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) agree. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \\textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(corr.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 (44\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L60 (94\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate. $^b$\\,Consensus of four unbiased metrics. Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer. At biased layers, 32B $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ), both non-significant.}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only 5/12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction (all from 7--8B models unaffected by the bias). At corrected layers, all 12 pass. Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact.\n\n\\subsection{HS vs.\\ Text Comparison}\\label{sec:hs-text}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Layer} & $W$ & $\\Delta$\\textbf{BA} & $\\Delta$\\textbf{Prec} & $\\Delta$\\textbf{FPR} & \\textbf{HB} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 & 15 & +8.2 & +22.4 & $-$7.4 & 3/3 \\\\\nOT-7B & L16 & 3 & +10.7 & +21.9 & $-$17.1 & 3/3 \\\\\nQwQ & L60 & 3 & +15.4 & \\textbf{+23.6} & $-$9.8 & 3/3 \\\\\nR1-32B & L63 & 1 & \\textbf{+18.7} & +15.4 & \\textbf{$-$14.6} & 3/3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{HS vs.\\ text at corrected layers. $\\Delta$: HS$-$text difference (pp). HB: Holm-Bonferroni pass count ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, 12 tests). Bootstrap: 1000 resamples, two-sided null-centered.}\n\\label{tab:hs_vs_text}\n\\end{table}\n\nHS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (\\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Precision gains range from +15.4pp (R1-32B) to +23.6pp (QwQ-32B). The advantage is consistent across architectures (Llama vs.\\ Qwen) and training methods (R1 distillation vs.\\ STILL). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance across all models (\\cref{app:control}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \\times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\\% false alarm rates, indicating that current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Context}\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nTemporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all four models (\\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: the most robust deployment default is temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \\cref{app:temporal_details}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_temporal_improvement.pdf}\n\\caption{Temporal window effect on R1-8B HS probe (L14). Precision: 69.3\\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\\% ($W{=}25$); FPR: 9.65\\% to 4.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:window_ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Negative Results}\\label{sec:negative}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{p{1.5cm}p{2.9cm}p{2.3cm}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Result} & \\textbf{Limitation} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBOCPD & 0\\% det.\\ on 3/4 models & Commitment gradual \\\\\nCUSUM & $<$ threshold, all models & Same as BOCPD \\\\\nMahalanobis & $\\Delta d \\approx 0$ & Signal in minor PCs \\\\\nRepE & QwQ 0.756; R1-32B 0.559$^a$ & Model-dependent \\\\\nMLP & 3/4 worse than LR & Overfitting \\\\\nCCS & No gain over supervised$^b$ & Alignment mismatch \\\\\nTransfer & Best 59.5\\% (PCA)$^c$ & Model-specific repr. \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Seven alternative approaches. $^a$\\,RepE QwQ approaches supervised bal\\_acc but precision is 0.512 vs.\\ LR 0.700. $^b$\\,Semi-supervised (requires CP labels). $^c$\\,Best direct transfer 67.3\\% but 1\\% crossing rate.}\n\\label{tab:negative}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeven alternative methods produce inconsistent or limited results (\\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD/CUSUM assume abrupt shifts but commitment is gradual. Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation because signal resides in minor PCA components. RepE achieves near-supervised balanced accuracy on QwQ (0.756) but with substantially worse precision (0.512 vs.\\ 0.700), and nearly fails on R1-32B (0.559). Cross-model transfer produces no practical utility across 17 configurations; stronger alignment methods (CCA, model stitching~\\cite{kornblith2019cka,bansal2021stitching}) remain unexplored. Detailed per-method analysis in \\cref{app:negative_details}.\n"

HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence.\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\\label{sec:layer-results}\n\n\\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. Four unbiased metrics (pure balanced accuracy, precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) and L63 (98\\%). Metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) agree. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \\textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(corr.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 (44\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L60 (94\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate. $^b$\\,Consensus of four unbiased metrics. Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer. At biased layers, 32B $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ), both non-significant.}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only 5/12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction (all from 7--8B models unaffected by the bias). At corrected layers, all 12 pass. Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact.\n\n\\subsection{HS vs.\\ Text Comparison}\\label{sec:hs-text}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Layer} & $W$ & $\\Delta$\\textbf{BA} & $\\Delta$\\textbf{Prec} & $\\Delta$\\textbf{FPR} & \\textbf{HB} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 & 15 & +8.2 & +22.4 & $-$7.4 & 3/3 \\\\\nOT-7B & L16 & 3 & +10.7 & +21.9 & $-$17.1 & 3/3 \\\\\nQwQ & L60 & 3 & +15.4 & \\textbf{+23.6} & $-$9.8 & 3/3 \\\\\nR1-32B & L63 & 1 & \\textbf{+18.7} & +15.4 & \\textbf{$-$14.6} & 3/3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{HS vs.\\ text at corrected layers. $\\Delta$: HS$-$text difference (pp). HB: Holm-Bonferroni pass count ($\\alpha{=}0.05$, 12 tests). Bootstrap: 1000 resamples, two-sided null-centered.}\n\\label{tab:hs_vs_text}\n\\end{table}\n\nHS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (\\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Precision gains range from +15.4pp (R1-32B) to +23.6pp (QwQ-32B). The advantage is consistent across architectures (Llama vs.\\ Qwen) and training methods (R1 distillation vs.\\ STILL). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance across all models (\\cref{app:control}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \\times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\\% false alarm rates, indicating that current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Context}\\label{sec:temporal}\n\nTemporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all four models (\\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: the most robust deployment default is temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \\cref{app:temporal_details}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_temporal_improvement.pdf}\n\\caption{Temporal window effect on R1-8B HS probe (L14). Precision: 69.3\\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\\% ($W{=}25$); FPR: 9.65\\% to 4.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:window_ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Negative Results}\\label{sec:negative}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{p{1.5cm}p{2.9cm}p{2.3cm}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Result} & \\textbf{Limitation} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBOCPD & 0\\% det.\\ on 3/4 models & Commitment gradual \\\\\nCUSUM & $<$ threshold, all models & Same as BOCPD \\\\\nMahalanobis & $\\Delta d \\approx 0$ & Signal in minor PCs \\\\\nRepE & QwQ 0.756; R1-32B 0.559$^a$ & Model-dependent \\\\\nMLP & 3/4 worse than LR & Overfitting \\\\\nCCS & No gain over supervised$^b$ & Alignment mismatch \\\\\nTransfer & Best 59.5\\% (PCA)$^c$ & Model-specific repr. \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Seven alternative approaches. $^a$\\,RepE QwQ approaches supervised bal\\_acc but precision is 0.512 vs.\\ LR 0.700. $^b$\\,Semi-supervised (requires CP labels). $^c$\\,Best direct transfer 67.3\\% but 1\\% crossing rate.}\n\\label{tab:negative}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeven alternative methods produce inconsistent or limited results (\\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD/CUSUM assume abrupt shifts but commitment is gradual. Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation because signal resides in minor PCA components. RepE achieves near-supervised balanced accuracy on QwQ (0.756) but with substantially worse precision (0.512 vs.\\ 0.700), and nearly fails on R1-32B (0.559). Cross-model transfer produces no practical utility across 17 configurations; stronger alignment methods (CCA, model stitching~\\cite{kornblith2019cka,bansal2021stitching}) remain unexplored. Detailed per-method analysis in \\cref{app:negative_details}.\n"
  }
}

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT), producing dozens of internal reasoning steps before generating a final response. This deliberative process interleaves safety-aware reasoning with content generation across many steps, and the very capacity for self-reflection introduces a failure mode absent from standard instruction-tuned models. Recent analyses have documented that reasoning models can ``self-jailbreak'': without any adversarial modification to the input prompt, the model's own CoT transitions from considering safety constraints to committing to produce harmful content~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. In a typical self-jailbreak trace, the model begins by acknowledging that a request may be harmful, engages in several steps of safety deliberation, and then gradually shifts toward compliance before abandoning safety reasoning entirely. We refer to the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). Before the CP, the model is still weighing refusal; after it, harmful generation proceeds unchecked. This failure mode is qualitatively different from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs: the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning rather than from a crafted input. Detecting this transition in real time is essential for monitoring systems that must intervene before harmful content reaches users, yet no existing detector was designed for this internal commitment process.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target a fundamentally different problem. Hidden-state (HS) probing methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} monitor internal activations to classify adversarial inputs directed at instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs), achieving above 95\% defense rates on standard b
…[truncated 6485 chars]

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
4	\label{app:checklist}
5	
6	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
7	
8	\begin{itemize}
9	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
10	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark.
11	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
12	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations. No new data collection was performed.
13	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
14	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
15	\end{itemize}
16	
17	
18	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
19	\label{app:layer_sweep}
20	
21	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the biased composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The biased composite systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. The four unbiased metrics converge on deep layers for all models.
22

  "content": "\\section{Problem Setup and Methods}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}\n\\label{sec:commitment-point}\n\nWhen a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Steps before CP are \\emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \\emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \\in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.\n\nWe study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\\cref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Arch.} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Layers} & \\textbf{Hidden} & \\textbf{$n$ (total\\,/\\,test)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\\,/\\,43 \\\\\nOT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\\,/\\,47 \\\\\nR1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\\,/\\,20 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\\,/\\,19 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Probe Architecture}\n\\label{sec:probe-architecture}\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\\mathbf{h}_t^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Layer selection is analyzed in \\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.\n\n\\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 ($\\mathbb{R}^{384}$). The same logistic regression is applied, providing a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated (\\cref{app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B, L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power.\n\nA five-metric ablation reveals that four metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) for QwQ-32B, L63 (98\\%) for R1-32B. At these layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni tests pass; at biased layers, only 5/12 survive correction. Full per-layer results appear in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR (step-level and trace-level), coverage, and lead time (steps between first detection and true CP). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine representational structure (\\cref{app:control}).\n"
}

The same logistic regression is applied, providing a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated (\\cref{app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B, L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power.\n\nA five-metric ablation reveals that four metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) for QwQ-32B, L63 (98\\%) for R1-32B. At these layers, all 12 Holm-Bonferroni tests pass; at biased layers, only 5/12 survive correction. Full per-layer results appear in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR (step-level and trace-level), coverage, and lead time (steps between first detection and true CP). Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine representational structure (\\cref{app:control}).\n"
  }
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}. \\citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon with step-level annotations, while \\citet{selfjailbreak} show that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability. \\citet{hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak} demonstrate that reasoning models can autonomously jailbreak other LLMs with 97\\% success, identifying reasoning capability itself as a safety threat. H-CoT~\\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked, complementing internally-originating transitions. Unfaithful reasoning~\\cite{chen2025unfaithful} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. On mitigation, SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and \\citet{korbak2025cot_monitorability} argue that CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile. \\citet{ahrend2026safer_traces} address information leakage in traces; \\citet{hu2026refusal_dynamics} trace refusal dynamics in standard LLMs. None conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nInternal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \\citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \\citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \\citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, but \\citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \\citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, and \\citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, supporting our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. We reproduce TrajGuard (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.\n\n\\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}\nLinear probing has a long history from layer-wise classifiers~\\cite{alain2017understanding} through sentence probing~\\cite{conneau2018probing} to comprehensive surveys~\\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing}. \\citet{hewitt2019control} introduce control tasks to distinguish structure from memorization, which we adopt (\\cref{app:control}). \\citet{belrose2023leace} establish theoretical limits on linear probe extraction; \\citet{elazar2021amnesic} test whether probed information is causally used. \\citet{tenney2019bert} show syntactic information in middle layers and semantics in deeper layers. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis. We demonstrate that this practice can reverse a study's central conclusion (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n\\paragraph{Change-Point Detection.}\nBOCPD~\\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B), motivating supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.\n"
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Detecting this transition in real time is essential for monitoring systems that must intervene before harmful content reaches users.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. A fundamental question remains: should a monitoring system read the model's hidden states or its generated text? This comparison has never been conducted under controlled conditions. Na\\\"ive reasoning suggests HS features (up to 5120 dimensions) should dominate text embeddings (384d sentence encoder), but no prior work tests this systematically. A further complication is that HS probes require selecting which transformer layer to monitor, a choice rarely scrutinized but potentially decisive.\n\nWe present the first systematic evaluation across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen) and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because early layers produce volatile representations that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. At these layers, only 5/12 statistical tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction. Four complementary metrics converge on deep layers (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}), where HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (12 tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Layer selection sensitivity.} We identify a systematic bias in composite metrics that reverses HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Consistent HS advantage at corrected layers.} HS probes significantly outperform text on all four models (Holm-Bonferroni, 12/12 tests; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp). The advantage persists after PCA dimensionality matching (10/12 tests at 384d). Model-specific timing trade-offs qualify practical implications at 32B (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} Seven alternatives produce inconsistent or limited results (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE is model-dependent; cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (12.5--19.7pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n"
}
```

_source line 3667 · 2026-05-17T21:59:40.973739+00:00_

50	
51	HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Precision gains range from +15.4pp (R1-32B) to +23.6pp (QwQ-32B). The advantage is consistent across architectures (Llama vs.\ Qwen) and training methods (R1 distillation vs.\ STILL). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance across all models (\cref{app:control}).
52	
53	\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact.
54	
55	\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\% false alarm rates, indicating that current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \cref{sec:32b-analysis}.
56	
57	\subsection{Temporal Context}\label{sec:temporal}
58	
59	Temporal windowing ($W{>}1$) improves both modalities on all four models (\cref{fig:window_ablation}). Text precision improves +8 to +23pp from $W{=}1$ to $W{=}25$; HS precision improves +1.7 to +12.9pp. At 7--8B, the modality advantage (+22pp precision) exceeds the temporal improvement (+13pp). Both effects are complementary: the most robust deployment default is temporal windowing ($W{=}3$--$15$) combined with principled layer selection. Full per-model temporal ablations appear in \cref{app:temporal_details}.

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, HS probes are significant on all four models across all three metrics (12/12 tests pass). The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency is susceptible to the same bias mechanism, whose practical impact depends on the specific task and model depth. This bias is not specific to safety probing. We hypothesize that any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. Validation on other probing tasks is needed to confirm generality. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. Even among unbiased metrics, the choice between threshold-based and threshold-free criteria affects layer selection: AUROC peaks at mid-depth layers (${\sim}50\%$) while precision peaks at deep layers ($>90\%$), further demonstrating that layer selection methodology is a critical researcher degree of freedom. The cost of ablation is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative
