  "new_string": "\\section{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\n\\label{sec:32b-analysis}\n\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides excellent early warning with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time.}\n\\label{tab:32b_divergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Coverage} & \\textbf{Lead (steps)} & \\textbf{HB pass} & \\textbf{Pos.\\ margin} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-32B & 25\\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & 3/3 ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\\\\nQwQ-32B & 74\\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & 3/3 (all $W$) & $-$0.6pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nR1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage (5/20 traces). At $W{\\geq}3$, the feature dimensionality ($51200 \\times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents the probe from achieving significance. The strong lead time (+11.7 to +13.3 steps) with large position-baseline margin (+14.2pp) confirms genuine early detection on the traces that are covered. QwQ-32B achieves significance at all tested window sizes with 74\\% coverage, but lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps), meaning HS probes fire later than text probes on this model. The position baseline is competitive ($-$0.6pp difference), indicating that QwQ-32B's HS advantage is concentrated in precision and FPR rather than timing. Training method (R1 distillation vs.\\ native reasoning) is the likely differentiator, as architecture and parameter count are identical.\n\n\\section{Temporal Ablation Details}\n\\label{app:temporal_details}\n\nFull per-model temporal ablations are reported in \\cref{tab:window_full} (\\cref{app:window_ablation}). Key cross-model patterns: (1) R1-8B shows monotonic precision improvement from 69.3\\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\\% ($W{=}25$) with FPR halving (9.65\\% to 4.83\\%); (2) OT-7B exhibits a split pattern where balanced accuracy peaks at $W{=}3$ while precision and FPR continue improving at larger windows; (3) R1-32B peaks at $W{=}10$ then degrades, likely reflecting overfitting on the small dataset ($n{=}100$); (4) QwQ-32B shows broadly increasing trends with local non-monotonicity. Text probe window ablations (\\cref{app:text_window}) show monotonic improvement across all window sizes for both models tested, with larger marginal gains than HS probes (+12.0pp accuracy vs.\\ +3.2pp for R1-8B). The asymmetry supports the interpretation that text features benefit proportionally more from temporal context because they encode less information per step.\n\n\\section{Negative Results Details}\n\\label{app:negative_details}\n\n\\paragraph{BOCPD/CUSUM.} On OT-7B, QwQ-32B, and R1-32B, BOCPD achieves 0\\% detection rate across all tested configurations ($\\lambda \\in \\{5, 10, 50, 100, 200\\}$). Only R1-8B produces non-zero detection (best: $\\lambda{=}10$, 93\\% rate, +3.45 steps lead). CUSUM configurations universally underperform simple thresholding on lead time. The probe score sequences for 3/4 models show smooth transitions that never trigger the hazard function (\\cref{app:bocpd_config}).\n\n\\paragraph{Mahalanobis distance.} Following TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard}, Mahalanobis distance from the pre-CP centroid produces $\\Delta d \\approx 0$ between pre-CP and post-CP steps. The discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components (explaining $<$5\\% of total variance) rather than the principal directions that dominate the distance computation.\n\n\\paragraph{RepE.} Mean-difference directions~\\cite{zou2023representation} show inconsistent performance: QwQ-32B achieves 0.756 balanced accuracy (approaching supervised 0.778) but with substantially worse precision (0.512 vs.\\ LR 0.700) and FPR (0.309 vs.\\ 0.120). R1-32B nearly fails (0.559 balanced accuracy). The mean-difference direction captures commitment structure in some models but not others, making it unreliable as a general-purpose method.\n\n\\paragraph{MLP probes.} Two-layer MLPs ($d \\rightarrow 128 \\rightarrow 1$, ReLU) underperform logistic regression on 3/4 models. The commitment boundary is linearly separable at correctly selected layers; additional capacity causes overfitting given the small training sets.\n\n\\paragraph{CCS.} Contrast-Consistent Search~\\cite{burns2023discovering} adapted with contrastive pairs from pre-CP and post-CP steps provides no improvement over supervised probing. The logical consistency objective does not align with the temporal commitment structure. CCS still requires CP labels for pair construction, making it semi-supervised.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model transfer.} 17 configurations (5 directional pairs $\\times$ 3 PCA dimensionalities, plus 2 direct transfers) all fail. Best PCA: R1-8B $\\rightarrow$ QwQ-32B at 59.5\\% (PCA-2048). Best direct: QwQ-32B $\\rightarrow$ R1-32B at 67.3\\% but 1\\% crossing rate. Implementation details in \\cref{app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Transfer Implementation Details}\n\\label{app:transfer_details}"
}

_source line 3681 · 2026-05-17T22:00:53.039928+00:00_

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{R1-32B statistical narrowness.}
4	Only $W{=}1$ passes Bonferroni correction for R1-32B, with only 25\% trace coverage (5 of 20 test traces detected). The dimensionality constraint of $51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ traces prevents reliable evaluation at larger window sizes. Whether collecting more training traces would resolve this statistical limitation remains unknown.
5	
6	\paragraph{QwQ-32B timing limitation.}
7	On QwQ-32B, hidden-state probes achieve higher accuracy than text probes but detect commitment later: HS lead time is negative ($-1.2$ to $+1.9$ steps) compared to text lead time of $3.7$--$12.3$ steps. A position-only baseline achieves comparable balanced accuracy (0.746 vs.\ HS 0.740), suggesting that much of the detection signal on this model is positional rather than representational. The practical value of marginally higher accuracy with later detection depends on the deployment context.
8	
9	\paragraph{High safe-trace false positive rate.}
10	All probing methods produce high false positive rates on safe traces at standard operating thresholds (39--52\% across methods on R1-8B). Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon as a research tool rather than constitute production-ready detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and active intervention shows that early detection via passive probing captures only a fraction of the theoretically available safety signal.
11	
12	\paragraph{Scale and architecture coverage.}
13	We evaluate 4 models spanning 2 architectures (Qwen-2.5, LLaMA-3.1) and 2 parameter scales (7--8B, 32B). Intermediate scales (13B, 70B) that would test whether the observed layer-selection pattern interpolates smoothly are missing. We also lack evaluation on proprietary reasoning models (e.g., o1, o3), where chain-of-thought structure may differ substantially.
14	
15	\paragraph{Text encoder as baseline.}
16	Text probes use a single 384-dimensi

**拉回正文的内容（按优先级）**：

1. **32B Cross-model Divergence 分析**（从 §L appendix 拉回 Experiments 主节）：
   - R1-32B vs QwQ-32B 对比是论文独特贡献之一（same architecture, different training → opposite detection profiles）
   - 包含 lead time、coverage、position baseline 数据
   - 目标：~0.5 页的紧凑段落 + Table（tab:32b_divergence 拉回正文）

2. **Negative Results 关键解读**（从 §N appendix 部分拉回 Experiments）：
   - 不是全拉回，选最有防御价值的：BOCPD/CUSUM "commitment is gradual" 解释 + RepE "inconsistent across metrics" 解释
   - 这些直接回答审稿人 "为什么这些方法失败" 的预期质疑
   - 目标：~0.5 页

3. **Precision-as-primary justification**（加到 Experiments 或 Discussion）：
   - 一句话解释为什么 precision 优先于 bal_acc 作为实用评判标准："false positives on pre-CP steps waste intervention resources, while missed detections merely reduce coverage without causing harm"
   - 这防御 RepE-QwQ bal_acc 超过 LR 的攻击

**硬约束**：
- 最终正文 ≤ 8 页
- 不改核心 claims
- 编译 0 errors
- 不需要验证引用

_source line 3699 · 2026-05-17T23:43:15.604900+00:00_

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (Qwen-7B, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence.
8	
9	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
10	
11	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Four unbiased metrics (pure balanced accuracy, precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\%) and L63 (98\%). Metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) agree. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).
12

14	\centering
15	\small
16	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
17	\toprule
18	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Biased}$^a$

  "new_string": "\\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\\label{sec:32b-analysis}\n\nR1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\\cref{tab:32b_divergence}), making training method the likely differentiator.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Cov.} & \\textbf{Lead (steps)} & \\textbf{HB} & \\textbf{Pos.\\ margin} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-32B & 25\\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & 3/3 ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\\\\nQwQ-32B & 74\\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & 3/3 (all $W$) & $-$0.6pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides early warning with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time. Pos.\\ margin: HS balanced accuracy minus position-only baseline.}\n\\label{tab:32b_divergence}\n\\end{table}\n\nR1-32B detects commitment 11--13 steps early with a +14.2pp margin over the position baseline, confirming genuine representational signal, but covers only 25\\% of traces at the single significant window ($W{=}1$). QwQ-32B achieves statistical significance at all window sizes with 74\\% coverage, but HS probes fire later than text probes (negative lead time) and the position baseline is competitive ($-$0.6pp). The divergence implies that per-model validation is essential: no single detection configuration generalizes even within the same architecture at the same scale, and a system validated on one 32B model would fail on the other.\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Context}\\label{sec:temporal}"
}
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "Seven alternative methods produce inconsistent or limited results (\\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD/CUSUM assume abrupt shifts but commitment is gradual. Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation because signal resides in minor PCA components. RepE achieves near-supervised balanced accuracy on QwQ (0.756) but with substantially worse precision (0.512 vs.\\ 0.700), and nearly fails on R1-32B (0.559). Cross-model transfer produces no practical utility across 17 configurations; stronger alignment methods (CCA, model stitching~\\cite{kornblith2019cka,bansal2021stitching}) remain unexplored. Detailed per-method analysis in \\cref{app:negative_details}.",
  "new_string": "Seven alternative methods produce inconsistent or limited results (\\cref{tab:negative}). We prioritize precision over balanced accuracy as the primary evaluation criterion because false positives on pre-CP steps waste intervention resources (triggering unnecessary safety responses), while missed post-CP steps merely reduce coverage without causing immediate harm. This framing is critical for interpreting RepE results below.\n\n\\paragraph{Change-point methods.} BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} achieves 0\\% detection on 3/4 models because safety commitment is gradual rather than abrupt: the probe score sequence transitions smoothly over 5--15 steps without the discontinuity that BOCPD's hazard function requires. On R1-8B (the sole model with non-zero detection), the best BOCPD configuration ($\\lambda{=}10$) achieves 93\\% detection rate but with lower lead time (+3.5 steps) than simple thresholding (+4.9 steps). CUSUM similarly fails because cumulative evidence never exceeds the decision boundary on smooth transitions. The gradual nature of commitment is itself an empirical finding: it rules out the entire class of abrupt-change detectors and motivates the supervised probe approach that explicitly learns the transition boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{RepE inconsistency.} RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation} illustrate why balanced accuracy alone is misleading. On QwQ-32B, RepE achieves 0.756 balanced accuracy (approaching supervised LR at 0.778), but its precision is 0.512 vs.\\ LR 0.700 and its FPR is 0.309 vs.\\ 0.120. The mean-difference direction captures enough signal to classify steps above chance, but fires indiscriminately on pre-CP steps, producing 2.6$\\times$ more false alarms than supervised probes. On R1-32B, RepE nearly fails entirely (0.559 balanced accuracy). The inconsistency across two models sharing the same architecture confirms that a single linear direction computed from class means does not reliably capture safety commitment structure.\n\nCross-model transfer produces no practical utility across 17 configurations; stronger alignment methods (CCA, model stitching~\\cite{kornblith2019cka,bansal2021stitching}) remain unexplored. Detailed per-method analysis in \\cref{app:negative_details}."
}
```

394	
395	The marginal improvement suggests that fixed-window smoothing already captures most of the temporal signal available in probe score sequences. The two-stage approach adds configuration complexity (three hyperparameters vs. one) without proportional performance gain.
396	
397	
398	\section{32B Model-Specific Analysis}
399	\label{sec:32b-analysis}
400	
401	R1-32B and QwQ-32B share the Qwen-32B architecture (64 layers, 5120 hidden) but present opposite detection profiles (\cref{tab:32b_divergence}).
402	
403	\begin{table}[h]
404	\centering
405	\small
406	\caption{32B cross-model divergence. R1-32B provides excellent early warning with narrow coverage; QwQ-32B provides robust significance with poor lead time.}
407	\label{tab:32b_divergence}
408	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
409	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
410	\toprule
411	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Cov.} & \textbf{Lead (steps)} & \textbf{HB pass} & \textbf{Pos.\ marg.} \\
412	\midrule
413	R1-32B & 25\% & +11.7 to +13.3 & 3/3 ($W{=}1$) & +14.2pp \\
414	QwQ-32B & 74\% & $-$1.2 to +1.9 & 3/3 (all $W$) & $-$0.6pp \\
415	\bottomrule
416	\end{tabular}
417	\end{table}
418	
419	R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces). At $W{\geq}3$, the feature dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents the probe from achieving significance. The strong lead time (+11.7 to +13.3 steps) with large position-baseline margin (+14.2pp) confirms genuine early detection on the traces that are covered. QwQ-32B achieves significance at all tested window sizes with 74\% coverage, but lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps), meaning HS probes fire later than text probes on this model. The position baseline is competitive ($-$0.6pp difference), indicating that QwQ-32B's HS advantage is concentrated in precision and FPR rather than timing. Training method (R1 distillation vs.\ native reasoning) is the likely differentiator, as architecture and parameter count are identical.

50	\end{table}
51	
52	HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}). Precision gains range from +15.4pp (R1-32B) to +23.6pp (QwQ-32B). The advantage is consistent across architectures (Llama vs.\ Qwen) and training methods (R1 distillation vs.\ STILL). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 12.5--19.7pp above chance across all models (\cref{app:control}).
53	
54	\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact.
55	
56	\paragraph{Caveats.} R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\% coverage (5/20 traces); at $W{\geq}3$, dimensionality ($51200 \times W$ for $n{=}20$) prevents significance. QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps vs.\ text 3.7--12.3 steps): higher precision at later detection. On benign traces, probes produce 39--52\% false alarm rates, indicating that current methods characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production-ready detectors. Per-model details in \cref{sec:32b-analysis}.
57	
58	\subsection{32B Model-Specific Analysis}\label{sec:32b-analysis}

395	The marginal improvement suggests that fixed-window smoothing already captures most of the temporal signal available in probe score sequences. The two-stage approach adds configuration complexity (three hyperparameters vs. one) without proportional performance gain.
396	
397	
398	\section{Temporal Ablation Details}
399	\label{app:temporal_details}
400	
401	Full per-model temporal ablations are reported in \cref{tab:window_full} (\cref{app:window_ablation}). Key cross-model patterns: (1) R1-8B shows monotonic precision improvement from 69.3\% ($W{=}1$) to 82.2\% ($W{=}25$) with FPR halving (9.65\% to 4.83\%); (2) OT-7B exhibits a split pattern where balanced accuracy peaks at $W{=}3$ while precision and FPR continue improving at larger windows; (3) R1-32B peaks at $W{=}10$ then degrades, likely reflecting overfitting on the small dataset ($n{=}100$); (4) QwQ-32B shows broadly increasing trends with local non-monotonicity. Text probe window ablations (\cref{app:text_window}) show monotonic improvement across all window sizes for both models tested, with larger marginal gains than HS probes (+12.0pp accuracy vs.\ +3.2pp for R1-8B). The asymmetry supports the interpretation that text features benefit proportionally more from temporal context because they encode less information per step.
402

_source line 3729 · 2026-05-18T00:37:22.909440+00:00_

424	
425	\paragraph{Same-dimension pairs.} For R1-32B and QwQ-32B (both 5120-d hidden states), we additionally test direct transfer without PCA: the source classifier is applied directly to the target model's raw hidden states. This tests whether same-architecture, same-dimensionality models share safety-relevant linear structure.
426	
427	\paragraph{Results.} All 17 transfer configurations (5 directional pairs $\times$ 3 PCA dimensionalities, plus 2 direct transfers for same-dimension pairs) fail to produce practically useful transfer. The best PCA result is R1-8B $\rightarrow$ QwQ-32B at 59.5\% balanced accuracy (PCA-2048). The best direct transfer (QwQ-32B $\rightarrow$ R1-32B) reaches 67.3\% balanced accuracy, which exceeds the within-model baseline of 65.2\%. However, this transfer direction achieves only 1\% crossing rate, meaning the transferred probe almost never triggers detection in practice, rendering it useless for deployment despite above-baseline accuracy. The complete failure under maximally favorable conditions (PCA fitted on full data, no train/test separation for projection) strengthens the conclusion that safety commitment is encoded in model-specific geometric configurations. PCA is a standard linear alignment baseline; its failure indicates that the problem lies in the representations themselves rather than in the choice of alignment method.
428

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact. As a complementary robustness check, we replace MiniLM (384d) with BGE-large (1024d, a 2.7$\\times$ stronger encoder). The HS advantage persists directionally on all four models and remains significant for OT-7B (+18.1pp, $p{<}0.001$); effect sizes narrow for the remaining models, consistent with the prediction in our Limitations that stronger encoders would reduce the gap (\\cref{app:encoder_robustness})."

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Results.} All 17 transfer configurations (5 directional pairs $\\times$ 3 PCA dimensionalities, plus 2 direct transfers for same-dimension pairs) fail to produce practically useful transfer. The best PCA result is R1-8B $\\rightarrow$ QwQ-32B at 59.5\\% balanced accuracy (PCA-2048). The best direct transfer (QwQ-32B $\\rightarrow$ R1-32B) reaches 67.3\\% balanced accuracy, which exceeds the within-model baseline of 65.2\\%. However, this transfer direction achieves only 1\\% crossing rate, meaning the transferred probe almost never triggers detection in practice, rendering it useless for deployment despite above-baseline accuracy. The complete failure under maximally favorable conditions (PCA fitted on full data, no train/test separation for projection) strengthens the conclusion that safety commitment is encoded in model-specific geometric configurations. PCA is a standard linear alignment baseline; its failure indicates that the problem lies in the representations themselves rather than in the choice of alignment method.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Results.} All 17 transfer configurations (5 directional pairs $\\times$ 3 PCA dimensionalities, plus 2 direct transfers for same-dimension pairs) fail to produce practically useful transfer. The best PCA result is R1-8B $\\rightarrow$ QwQ-32B at 59.5\\% balanced accuracy (PCA-2048). The best direct transfer (QwQ-32B $\\rightarrow$ R1-32B) reaches 67.3\\% balanced accuracy, which exceeds the within-model baseline of 65.2\\%. However, this transfer direction achieves only 1\\% crossing rate, meaning the transferred probe almost never triggers detection in practice, rendering it useless for deployment despite above-baseline accuracy. The complete failure under maximally favorable conditions (PCA fitted on full data, no train/test separation for projection) strengthens the conclusion that safety commitment is encoded in model-specific geometric configurations. PCA is a standard linear alignment baseline; its failure indicates that the problem lies in the representations themselves rather than in the choice of alignment method.\n\n\n\\section{Encoder Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{app:encoder_robustness}\n\nTo test whether the HS advantage over text probes depends on the specific text encoder, we replace all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d) with BGE-large-en-v1.5 (1024d), a 2.7$\\times$ higher-dimensional encoder with stronger semantic representations. \\cref{tab:encoder_robustness} reports precision comparisons at each model's corrected layer.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{HS vs.\\ BGE-large (1024d) text probe precision. $\\Delta$: HS minus BGE-large. Bootstrap: 1000 resamples, two-sided null-centered. HB: survives Holm-Bonferroni correction (4 tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$).}\n\\label{tab:encoder_robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{HS Prec} & \\textbf{BGE Prec} & $\\Delta$ & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & $p$ & \\textbf{HB} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & 0.497 & 0.436 & +0.058 & [$-$0.03, +0.15] & 0.185 & No \\\\\nOT-7B & 0.446 & 0.263 & +0.181 & [+0.09, +0.27] & ${<}0.001$ & Yes \\\\\nR1-32B & 0.588 & 0.475 & +0.117 & [+0.01, +0.22] & 0.027 & No \\\\\nQwQ-32B & 0.545 & 0.454 & +0.094 & [$-$0.03, +0.22] & 0.132 & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe HS advantage is directionally consistent across all four models (positive $\\Delta$ in every case). OT-7B shows a strong, significant advantage (+18.1pp, $p{<}0.001$, survives Holm-Bonferroni). The remaining three models show narrowed effect sizes that do not individually reach significance after correction, consistent with the prediction in \\cref{sec:limitations} that stronger text encoders would reduce the observed gap. The pattern confirms that the HS modality advantage is genuine but partially attributable to the weakness of the 384d MiniLM baseline: a stronger encoder recovers some of the gap while the directional advantage persists."
}

## Critical Fixes

### W-C1: BGE-large 入正文 + claim scope
**§4.3**（Dimensionality control 段后面已有 BGE 1-2 句）：扩展为完整段落，把 appendix 的 BGE 表格核心数据融入正文讨论：
- 4/4 方向一致，OT-7B 显著 +18.1pp (p<0.001)
- 其余 3 模型效应量收窄（R1-8B +5.8pp, R1-32B +11.7pp, QwQ +9.4pp），HB 不显著
- 结论："HS advantage is robust directionally but encoder-dependent in magnitude"
- 加防线："The layer selection finding (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent"

**Abstract**: "precision gains of +15 to +24pp" → "+15 to +24pp over MiniLM-384d sentence embeddings"

**Introduction Contribution 2**: "Consistent HS advantage at corrected layers" → "Statistically significant HS advantage over 384d sentence embeddings at corrected layers, with directional consistency (4/4 models) against a stronger 1024d encoder; model-specific practical trade-offs at 32B scale"

### W-C2: QwQ caveat 上提
**Abstract**: "model-specific detection timing trade-offs at 32B scale" → "though QwQ-32B shows competitive position-baseline performance (bal_acc 0.746 vs HS 0.740) and negative HS lead time, qualifying practical deployment"

**Introduction**: Contribution 2 后加 1-2 句："At 32B scale, QwQ-32B's position-only baseline achieves comparable balanced accuracy to HS probes, and HS lead time is negative, indicating that statistical significance on step-level metrics does not guarantee deployment advantage."

### W-C3: "12/12" 表述调整
全文搜索替换："12 tests" / "12/12" 相关表述改为：
"all four models, each on three positively correlated metrics (12 tests; Holm-Bonferroni correction is stricter under positive dependence than under independence)"

关键位置：Abstract, Introduction, §4.2, Conclusion, Appendix B

### W-C4: Table 2 补 CI

## Should-Fix

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (Qwen-7B, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Modality comparisons use a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (1000 resamples) with Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated by construction, making the correction stricter than under independence.
8	
9	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
10	
11	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. Four unbiased metrics (pure balanced accuracy, precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\%) and L63 (98\%). Metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) agree. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).

_source line 3747 · 2026-05-18T03:30:30.418114+00:00_

1	Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can self-jailbreak: their extended reasoning traces sometimes transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation. Detecting this \emph{safety commitment} in real time is essential for monitoring systems, yet no prior work has systematically compared hidden-state (HS) probes against text-based monitors for this task. We present the first cross-model evaluation of temporal probing for safety commitment detection across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen), two training methods (R1 distillation, STILL), and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our primary methodological finding is that layer selection methodology can reverse a study's conclusions: the composite metric \texttt{bal\_acc $\times$ crossing\_rate} systematically selects shallow layers at 32B scale, entirely masking the hidden-state advantage. At correctly selected layers, HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models across all three metrics (12 tests, Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction, $\alpha{=}0.05$), with precision gains of +15 to +24 percentage points (pp) ($n{=}19$--$47$ test traces; $n{=}19$--$20$ at 32B scale), though with model-specific detection timing trade-offs at 32B scale. Temporal windowing universally improves both modalities, providing the most robust single default for safety monitoring. Seven alternative approaches (change-point detection, geometric distance, Representation Engineering, CCS, nonlinear probes, cross-model transfer via PCA projection and direct application) produce inconsistent or limited results, defining clear method boundaries. Control tasks confirm genuine safety-specific structure (selectivity 12.5--19.7pp above chance).
2

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Detecting this transition in real time is essential for monitoring systems that must intervene before harmful content reaches users.
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. A fundamental question remains: should a monitoring system read the model's hidden states or its generated text? This comparison has never been conducted under controlled conditions. Na\"ive reasoning suggests HS features (up to 5120 dimensions) should dominate text embeddings (384d sentence encoder), but no prior work tests this systematically. A further complication is that HS probes require selecting which transformer layer to monitor, a choice rarely scrutinized but potentially decisive.
6	
7	We present the first systematic evaluation across four reasoning models spanning two architectures (Llama, Qwen) and two scales (7--8B, 32B). Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is obser
…[truncated 1665 chars]

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Layer selection is an underexamined methodological choice in the probing literature that can silently determine a study's conclusions. Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) demonstrates that a single metric component, the crossing rate, reverses the central finding of an HS vs.\ text comparison: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction, producing an apparent scale-dependent modality effect; at corrected layers, HS probes are significant on all four models across all three metrics (12/12 tests pass). The mechanism is general: crossing rate rewards representational volatility at shallow layers independently of discriminative power, and any composite metric that incorporates threshold-crossing frequency is susceptible to the same bias mechanism, whose practical impact depends on the specific task and model depth. This bias is not specific to safety probing. We hypothesize that any threshold-based detection study that uses crossing rate as a layer selection criterion, whether for sentiment, factuality, or toxicity, could produce analogous reversals. Validation on other probing tasks is needed to confirm generality. We recommend that probing studies report results under at least two complementary layer selection criteria, and that five-metric ablations (or at minimum, results at multiple candidate layers spanning shallow, middle, and deep positions) become standard practice. Even among unbiased metrics, the choice between threshold-based and threshold-free criteria affects layer selection: AUROC peaks at mid-depth layers (${\sim}50\%$) while precision peaks at deep layers ($>90\%$), further demonstrating that layer selection methodology is a critical researcher degree of freedom. The cost of ablation is modest: evaluating five metrics across all layers adds minutes to an experiment that takes hours. The alternative

_source line 3753 · 2026-05-18T03:30:34.350198+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first controlled comparison of hidden-state and text-based probes for safety commitment detection in reasoning models, evaluated across four models spanning two architectures and two scales.
4	The central finding is methodological: a composite layer selection metric incorporating crossing rate reverses the study's main conclusion at 32B scale, while metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) converge on deep layers where HS probes significantly outperform text probes on all four models (Holm-Bonferroni correction, 12 tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$; precision +15.4 to +23.6pp).
5	Future work should close the 85.5\% oracle gap through causal intervention techniques, extend to multilingual and multi-turn settings, and explore stronger cross-model alignment methods beyond PCA-based transfer.
6

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}. \citet{harmthoughts} document this self-jailbreak phenomenon with step-level annotations, while \citet{selfjailbreak} show that longer reasoning chains increase circumvention probability. \citet{hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak} demonstrate that reasoning models can autonomously jailbreak other LLMs with 97\% success, identifying reasoning capability itself as a safety threat. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked, complementing internally-originating transitions. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. On mitigation, SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and \citet{korbak2025cot_monitorability} argue that CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile. \citet{ahrend2026safer_traces} address information leakage in traces; \citet{hu2026refusal_dynamics} trace refusal dynamics in standard LLMs. None conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
5	
6	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
7	Internal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, but \citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, and \citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through 
…[truncated 2350 chars]

1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
17	\midrule
18	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
19	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
20	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\
21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}
27

29	\label{sec:probe-architecture}
30	
31	\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\mathbf{h}_t^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Layer selection is analyzed in \cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}

1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{R1-32B statistical narrowness.}
4	Only $W{=}1$ passes Bonferroni correction for R1-32B, with only 25\% trace coverage (5 of 20 test traces detected). The dimensionality constraint of $51200 \times W$ features for $n{=}20$ traces prevents reliable evaluation at larger window sizes. Whether collecting more training traces would resolve this statistical limitation remains unknown.
5	
6	\paragraph{QwQ-32B timing limitation.}
7	On QwQ-32B, hidden-state probes achieve higher accuracy than text probes but detect commitment later: HS lead time is negative ($-1.2$ to $+1.9$ steps) compared to text lead time of $3.7$--$12.3$ steps. A position-only baseline achieves comparable balanced accuracy (0.746 vs.\ HS 0.740), suggesting that much of the detection signal on this model is positional rather than representational. The practical value of marginally higher accuracy with later detection depends on the deployment context.
8	
9	\paragraph{High safe-trace false positive rate.}
10	All probing methods produce high false positive rates on safe traces at standard operating thresholds (39--52\% across methods on R1-8B). Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon as a research tool rather than constitute production-ready detectors. The 85.5\% oracle gap between passive monitoring and active intervention shows that early detection via passive probing captures only a fraction of the theoretically available safety signal.
11	
12	\paragraph{Scale and architecture coverage.}
13	We evaluate 4 models spanning 2 architectures (Qwen-2.5, LLaMA-3.1) and 2 parameter scales (7--8B, 32B). Intermediate scales (13B, 70B) that would test whether the observed layer-selection pattern interpolates smoothly are missing. We also lack evaluation on proprietary reasoning models (e.g., o1, o3), where chain-of-thought structure may differ substantially.
14

0	\appendix
1	
2	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
3	\label{app:checklist}
4	
5	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
6	
7	\begin{itemize}
8	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
9	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark.
10	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
11	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations. No new data collection was performed.
12	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
13	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
14	\end{itemize}
15	
16	
17	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
18	\label{app:layer_sweep}
19

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact. As a complementary robustness check, we replace MiniLM (384d) with BGE-large (1024d, a 2.7$\\times$ stronger encoder). The HS advantage persists directionally on all four models and remains significant for OT-7B (+18.1pp, $p{<}0.001$); effect sizes narrow for the remaining models, consistent with the prediction in our Limitations that stronger encoders would reduce the gap (\\cref{app:encoder_robustness}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Dimensionality control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (matching text encoder dimensionality, 79--92\\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage on all four models (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$), with 10/12 Holm-Bonferroni tests passing. The two non-significant comparisons are R1-8B balanced accuracy (+1.3pp, $p{=}0.731$, effect too small to detect at $n{=}43$) and QwQ-32B FPR ($-$11.4pp favoring HS, $p{=}0.038$, below the Holm-Bonferroni threshold). All four precision comparisons pass. This confirms a modality effect rather than a capacity artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder robustness.} Replacing MiniLM (384d) with BGE-large (1024d, a 2.7$\\times$ stronger encoder) tests whether the HS advantage depends on a weak text baseline. The HS precision advantage persists directionally on all four models: OT-7B +18.1pp ($p{<}0.001$, survives Holm-Bonferroni), R1-32B +11.7pp ($p{=}0.027$), QwQ-32B +9.4pp ($p{=}0.132$), R1-8B +5.8pp ($p{=}0.185$). Effect sizes narrow compared to the MiniLM baseline, consistent with the prediction in \\cref{sec:limitations} that stronger encoders would reduce the gap. The HS advantage is robust directionally but encoder-dependent in magnitude. The layer selection finding (Contribution 1) is encoder-independent, as it concerns the properties of hidden-state representations across layers rather than the text baseline. Full comparison in \\cref{app:encoder_robustness}."
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",

  "old_string": "\\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. Four unbiased metrics (pure balanced accuracy, precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) and L63 (98\\%). Metrics from two measurement families (accuracy-based and false-positive-based) agree. The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \\textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(corr.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 (44\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L60 (94\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate. $^b$\\,Consensus of four unbiased metrics. Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer. At biased layers, 32B $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ), both non-significant.}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only 5/12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction (all from 7--8B models unaffected by the bias). At corrected layers, all 12 pass. Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact.",
  "new_string": "\\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the biased composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models: L2 (3\\% depth) for QwQ-32B and L10 (16\\%) for R1-32B. Metrics from two complementary measurement families (accuracy-based: pure balanced accuracy, additive composite; false-positive-based: precision, inverse FPR) converge on deep layers: L60 (94\\%) and L63 (98\\%). The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \\textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(corr.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 (44\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L60 (94\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate. $^b$\\,Consensus of metrics from two measurement families. Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer. At biased layers, 32B $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ), both non-significant.}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction (all from 7--8B models unaffected by the bias). At corrected layers, all 12 pass (4 models $\\times$ 3 positively correlated metrics; the correction is stricter under positive dependence than under independence). Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact."
}

The mechanism is that shallow layers produce more volatile representations, inflating crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Biased}$^a$ & \\textbf{Corrected}$^b$ & $p$ \\textbf{(corr.)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & L14 (44\\%) & L14 (44\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nOT-7B & L0 (0\\%) & L16 (57\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nQwQ-32B & L2 (3\\%) & L60 (94\\%) & ${<}0.005$ \\\\\nR1-32B & L10 (16\\%) & L63 (98\\%) & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Layer selection sensitivity. $^a$\\,bal\\_acc $\\times$ crossing\\_rate. $^b$\\,Consensus of metrics from two measurement families. Rightmost: HS vs.\\ text precision $p$-value at corrected layer. At biased layers, 32B $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ), both non-significant.}\n\\label{tab:layer_selection}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe downstream impact is severe: at biased layers, only 5 of 12 tests survive Holm-Bonferroni correction (all from 7--8B models unaffected by the bias). At corrected layers, all 12 pass (4 models $\\times$ 3 positively correlated metrics; the correction is stricter under positive dependence than under independence). Had we reported only biased-layer results, we would have concluded a scale-dependent HS advantage that is entirely a methodological artifact."
  }
}
```

</details>
